And there was Lukas, seventeen years old and already too big for his voice, sitting cross-legged on the packed earth with a whetstone in his hands, pretending to sharpen a stick.

"You're late," he said, without looking up.

"I had to hide from the dark," I said. It made sense in the dream. Everything makes sense in dreams.

"Hide from it?" He looked at me then, and his eyes were the amber I remembered, the amber that the years and the war and the impossible light had not been able to touch. "You don't hide from the dark, Fenris. You wait for it to blink."

The dream held that moment — held his face, held the light in the cave, held the unshakeable certainty of a nine-year-old boy who had not yet learned that the dark does not blink. And then the wheat returned, and the gold swallowed him, and I woke with the words still echoing in the quiet of my room.

You wait for it to blink.

I dreamed of wheat fields instead.

---

## Chapter 18: The Vanguard's Carol

The muster ground smelled of wet iron and cold ash.

Five hundred of them stood in the pre-dawn dark, arranged in ten ranks of fifty, and they made no sound at all. That was the first thing I noticed. Not the number. Not the black armor, matte and unmarked, or the iron masks with their narrow slits, or the way they held themselves with the trained stillness of creatures who had learned that stillness was a kind of weapon. The silence. Fifty assassins breathed in a way that did not disturb the air. Five hundred of them breathed, and the dawn mist lying low over the parade ground did not shiver.

I had been trained to notice that sort of thing. Seventeen years of it. A life spent learning to read the small deviations that told you a room had changed, a target had moved, a heartbeat had skipped. The absence of a sound was a sound. The absence of a sound was the loudest thing in the world if you had been made the way I had been made.

Vark stood at my right, a step back and half a pace to the side, the position he had occupied since I was eight years old and he had first walked me into a room full of men who wanted to know whether I was worth the grain it took to feed me. He was massive in the dark, shoulders like a yoke, his greatcoat settling around him like a hung curtain. He did not speak. Vark never spoke first at a muster. It was a kind of courtesy, or a kind of test, depending on the day.

The eastern towers of Vaelthorne began to edge the sky with grey. The rift-wells under the city hummed at the beck of that frequency, low and constant, a vibration you felt in the fillings of your teeth before you heard it with your ears. My wound, stitched by Thess the night before, pulled when I shifted my weight. I let it pull. Pain was a language I spoke fluently, and I had decided, somewhere in the small hours, that I would need it today.

Beyond the walls, beyond the Greywood, the world was doing something I had no name for. Reports had come in through the Auditor's threads for weeks — a slowing of the border raids, a loosening of the old hatreds, markets opening where armies had stood, a girl with a light in her and a boy with a crown in his future standing together in the bright places and calling it peace. The lords called it the Accord. I had heard the word spoken like a dirty thing in the Sanctum and like a holy thing in the streets. Whatever it was, it was the surface of the world now, and the surface was where Lukas stood, and I was down here in the dark with five hundred blades and a frequency that ate songs. That was the shape of my life. The brighter the world got above me, the deeper I was placed beneath it.

"Commander," Vark said.

It was the first word either of us had spoken. I stepped forward so that the five hundred could see my face.

They did not look at me. That was the second thing I noticed. Iron masks turned toward me in unison, the slits reflecting nothing, and I understood that they had been taught the same lesson I had been taught in the Pits: a weapon does not look at the hand that holds it. Looking was a kind of wanting, and wanting was a kind of weakness, and weakness got a thing recycled.

"I am Shadowfang," I said. My voice carried across the ground without effort. The conditioning had seen to that. "From this moment, I command this formation. You will obey me as you have obeyed Vark. You will obey me faster."

No reaction. Five hundred statues in the dark.

"Vark trained you to kill," I said. "I intend to teach you something else before I teach you that again."

A shift. Small. A fraction of a fraction of movement in the third rank — a chin lifting, perhaps, or a breath held differently. I noted it. The note-taker had noted it. The part of me that had been built to catalogue deviations recorded it without my asking and filed it away.

"You will learn to listen," I said.

That got me something. Not a sound — a change in the quality of the silence, the way a still pond changes when a fish turns beneath the surface. Five hundred assassins, nearly all of them certain that listening was what they already did. Listening was what hunters did. Listening was what predators did. They had been trained to hear the breath behind a door and the heartbeat through a wall. They did not know that what they called listening was only hearing sharpened to a point. They did not know the difference between hearing and listening, any more than a blade knows the difference between cutting and wounding.

I walked the ranks. I made a slow circuit of them, my boots crunching the frost, my hand trailing along the line of iron masks at shoulder height. Up close they smelled of oil and cold metal. Every one of them had a frequency. I could feel it the way a man feels heat off a stone — a small, tight note held behind the breastbone, the signature of a body compressed into a weapon and told it was a weapon and nothing more. Discord signatures, most of them. Vark's work. Good work. Cruel work. The kind of work I had been the raw material for.

I stopped in front of a figure in the second rank. Smaller than the others. Young.

"Your name," I said.

"Number forty-three," the figure said. The voice through the mask was flat, rehearsed.

"They took your name."

A pause. "Names are unnecessary, Commander."

"Names are the first thing they take," I said. "And the first thing they give back when they want you to do something you will not do for a number." I moved on. Behind me, I felt the silence tighten around that small figure like a hand closing.

I climbed the low platform at the head of the ground and turned to face them. The sky had gone from grey to a colour I had no name for — not night, not day, the thin skin between. The rift-wells beneath us sang their subsonic song in my bones. And under that, something else. Something I had been hearing for three nights and had not yet allowed myself to name.

A wrong note.

It lived in the Null-Quartz chamber below the training ground. I had felt it first as a pressure behind my eyes, then as a faint bitter taste — copper and old rain, the taste I had mistaken for my own mouth for seventeen years. It was not a sound exactly. It was the shape a sound would have if a sound could be made of absence. When I listened for it directly, it slipped away, the way a memory slips away when you reach for it too hard. When I stopped listening, it filled the space.

I had spent the night before the muster in the cave in the Wastes, holding the cold Oakhaven shard and listening to the Auditor explain the world in its flat, patient voice. Truth was justice. The rest was noise. The Voice had a hairline crack in it now, and through the crack something had leaked in that the Auditor had no category for. I had fallen asleep without certainty for the first time in seventeen years. I had dreamed of wheat and a cellar cave and a boy who told me not to hide from the dark.

You wait for it to blink.

"Close your eyes," I told the five hundred.

They did not move.

"That is an order."

Five hundred masks lowered, or rather, five hundred heads inclined a fraction, the eye-slits no longer facing me. I waited. I let the silence settle. I let the rift-well hum drop out of my awareness the way a man stops hearing the river after living a year beside it.

"Hear the rift-wells," I said. "Good. Now hear the space between two heartbeats. Not the heartbeat. The space."

Nothing. Five hundred breathing machines. I walked them through it slowly, the old methods, the fork and the frequency and the place where blood met bone. I had learned to listen in a room of Null-Quartz where the walls drank sound and the only thing left was the thing inside you. These five hundred had never been asked to listen to the space inside themselves. They had been asked to fill it.

"Again," I said. "The space is not empty. It has a texture. It has a temperature. Find it."

A long minute. Then — a ripple. One of them, deep in the seventh rank, shifted his weight. Another. The silence took on a quality I recognized: the quality of five hundred people hearing themselves for the first time and not liking what they heard.

"That is the noise you carry," I said. "That tight note behind your sternum. That is the machine's music. You have been told it is yours. It is not."

A voice spoke from the fourth rank. It came out before the man could stop it — a sergeant's voice, rough and certain. "With respect, Commander. It is ours. Commander Vark told us the warmth was proof. He told us it meant we were made for something."

The ranks went rigid. Every mask in the formation turned a fraction toward the fourth rank, and then toward me, waiting to see how a man is punished for speaking out of turn at a muster. I felt Vark go still behind me — not the stillness of approval but the stillness of a man watching a thing he had built being tested in front of him.

I did not punish him. That was the first command I gave them that they did not understand.

"He did tell you that," I said. "He told all of us. I believed him for seventeen years." I let the words carry across the ground. "I am not telling you he lied. I am telling you he did not know the whole of it. The warmth is real. The satisfaction is real. What it means is where he was wrong." I walked toward the fourth rank until I stood before the sergeant. "You feel it when you use the Discord. Like a door opening in your chest. Like the first breath after a long held one."

"Yes, Commander."

"That is an appetite," I said. "And something has been feeding it."

I let that sit. Vark, behind me, went very still.

"You were told you were blades," I said. "You were told the warmth you feel when you use the Discord is purpose. That it is proof you were made for something." I knew the feeling. I knew it the way a man knows a scar — the biological satisfaction of a body that had done what it was designed to do, the warm certainty that spread through the chest and settled behind the ribs like a contented animal. I had felt it a thousand times. I had felt it two nights ago, standing over a man I had killed in an alley off the Greywood road, and had spent the walk home trying not to think about the shape of his face as the light went out of it. "It is not purpose," I said. "It is an appetite. And something is feeding it."

I ended the drill there. I did not teach them killing that morning. I taught them to sit with the wrong note until they could hold it without flinching, and when the sun finally cleared the eastern towers and the mist burned off the ground, I dismissed them with a single word and watched them file away in perfect silence — but a different silence now. A listening silence. A silence with a crack in it.

Vark did not speak until the last of them had gone.

"You made them listen," he said.

"I made them listen."

"You told them the warmth was not theirs."

"It is not."

He turned to face me, and his great frame blocked the low sun, throwing me into shadow. His dark eyes — the eyes of the man who had shaped me, who knew my tells the way a sculptor knows the grain of stone — studied my face. I let him study it. I had nothing to hide in it that he had not put there himself.

"That note you taught them to hear," he said. "The one under the rift-wells. I have heard it too, Shadowfang. For years. I told myself it was the wells. You are telling me it is not."

"I am telling you I do not know what it is yet."

"But you are going to find out."

I said nothing. That was answer enough, and he knew it.

He stepped closer. "Three nights ago you left a clean window in a throne room. This morning you turn five hundred killers into listeners. Yesterday the lords' patrols ran the low quarters of the city and came back with names. This morning the patrol routes are redrawn." His voice stayed low, but it had a weight to it, the weight of a man who had been adding things up. "You are pulling back. From the civilians. From the kills. From the certainty."

"I am redrawing patrols onto the rift-wells and the empty wards," I said. "There is nothing in the low quarters worth a Vanguard patrol. The harvest gets what it needs from the wells."

"The harvest." He said the word as if tasting it. "That is what you are calling it now."

I should not have said that word in front of him. I knew it as soon as it left my mouth. The Auditor's terminology was not ours to speak aloud in the yard. But Vark was Vark, and he had heard it, and he was not a fool.

"Dismissed," I said.

He did not move. For a moment I thought he would press it — that he would take me by the shoulder, the way he had when I was eight and had tried to hold the Discord too long, and make me answer. But he only looked at me with those dark eyes, and something passed through his expression that I could not name, and then it was gone.

"The lords will ask why the low quarters went quiet," he said. "Give them a better answer than the truth."

Then he turned and walked away, and I stood alone on the platform in the thinning dark with the wrong note worrying at the edges of my hearing and the taste of copper and old rain gathering in my mouth.

I went down to the Null-Quartz chamber before the sun was fully up.

The chamber lay beneath the training ground, sealed behind a door of compressed Void-silver that only three people in Vaelthorne could open. I was one of them. The passage descended in a spiral cut through living rock, and as I went, the note grew stronger — no longer a pressure behind the eyes but a slow, patient pulling, as if something below were breathing in through the soles of my boots.

The door yielded to my hand. I stepped inside.

Null-Quartz is silence made solid. I had known it as a child, known it as the place where the fork had struck and the frequency had found the place where my blood met my bones and resonated until I screamed and the walls drank the scream. I knew it as the room where I had been made. Now I stood in it as the man it had made, and I listened.

The note was louder here. It was not one note. That was the first truth of it. It was many notes, layered one upon another, held in a suspension that should not have been possible — a thousand voices compressed into a single frequency, each one distinct, each one silenced, all of them singing together in a harmony so complete that it sounded like one vast, still chord. It was beautiful. That was the horror of it. It was the most beautiful sound I had ever heard, and it was made of the absence of sound, and every voice in it had been taken from someone who had once used that voice to say a name.

I picked one note out of the chord and held it — a high, thin thread with a wobble near the end, the note of someone who had been laughing when the machine found them. I had heard that wobble before. I had heard it in the market two nights earlier, in a girl's laughter that crossed the space between a human boy and a raven-kin child. The note in the chord was not that girl's — it was older, and the wobble had been compressed into a still, perfect thing — but it had the same shape, and that was enough. Somewhere, someone had once laughed exactly like that, and the machine had taken the laugh and pressed it flat and added it to its harmony, and the person who had made it was now a rounding error in an account no one would ever read.

The chamber held the memory of my own shaping. I stood where the figure with the cracked mask had stood, and I could almost see the tuning fork in the air, almost feel the frequency that had struck the place where my blood met my bones and made the Discord erupt like a bird from a cage that did not want to be opened. I had been the first. The Auditor's first instrument, its first successful filter, the raw ore that had become the blade. Every note in the chord had passed through a creature like me — a vessel, a receiver, a thing built to hold the song long enough to hand it over. I had spent seventeen years being the machine's finest mouth, and I was standing in its choir loft, hearing what I had sung.

I understood then what the Auditor had never told me in words. The harvest did not take lives. It took songs. It took the frequency that constituted a thing — the note a voice made saying a name, the note a forest made when it sang, the note a people made when they remembered — and it compressed that note into this chord, this still, perfect, silent harmony, and stored it. Finalized it. The Quiet Choir. The machinery beneath the rift-wells, beneath the market, beneath every place the lords had ever called a target. Every kill fed it. Every silence it made gave it more to hold. And the peace above me — the Accord, the markets, the bright rebuilding — was the loudest song of all, and the machine was hungry for it.

And I had been its finest instrument.

I stood in the dark chamber with the chord moving through my bones, and I felt the old warmth stir in my chest — the biological satisfaction of a body that had done what it was designed to do, recognizing the music of its own making. It rose in me like a familiar smell. Familiar. That was the worst of it. My body knew this frequency the way a drunk knows the taste of the bottle. It wanted it. It wanted to add its note to the chord.

I pressed my hand against the cold Null-Quartz wall until the stones bit into my palm, and I made myself listen to the voices underneath — the ones that were not mine, the ones that had been taken, the thousand small notes held in the still chord that were, each of them, someone's mother's lullaby, someone's name, someone's last word. And beneath them, almost too faint to hear, a phrase I almost recognized. A melody that rose too high where it should have reached, the way my mother's third phrase had always risen, the one she had reached for and never quite caught.

It was there. In the chord. The third phrase, held in the silence like a bird in a closed hand.

I did not catch it. But I heard that it was there.

When I came back up, the sun was over the towers and the city was moving through its morning — the bakery on Grief Row sliding stale loaves onto its sill, a shift of guards changing at the inner gate, the rift-wells humming their patient hum. I walked the upper walkway toward the records office with the taste of copper in my mouth and the chord still moving in my bones, and I made a decision that Vark had already seen coming.

I pulled the Vanguard off the civilians.

It was not mercy. I told myself that as I wrote the new patrol orders in the clean geometric script the lords required, and I did not believe it for a moment. Mercy was a word for people who still had a self to be merciful with. What I did was arithmetic. Every patrol that ran a low quarter produced three things: noise, names, and data. The noise fed the Vanguard's appetite. The names fed the ledger. The data fed the Choir. If I redirected five hundred listeners to the rift-wells and the empty wards and the sealed approaches where nothing lived, the machine got thinner. Not hungry — machines do not hunger. Less fed. Less able.

I wrote the orders carefully. I gave the lords a reason they would accept: civilian contact compromised the Discord signatures, muddied the harvest resonance, introduced unreliable frequencies into the wells. It was true enough. The Auditor would have accepted it. The Auditor did accept it — I felt the Voice stir in my skull, cooler than before, distant, as if the frequency that carried it had grown weaker and the signal had to travel farther to reach me.

Anomaly, it said. Not an accusation. A note in a ledger.

I finished the orders and sealed them and sent them up the chain, and by midday the low quarters of Vaelthorne were quiet in a way they had not been quiet in seventeen years. Mothers kept their children close and did not know why. The bakery sold twice its usual stale bread to men who did not know why they were hungry. The Choir, beneath it all, missed its notes and went on singing.

Thess sent for me in the late afternoon. Her message was a single line scratched on a scrap of parchment — *stitches holding?* — and I went down to her chambers in the lower levels because she had never once asked for me and I did not know what to make of it. She was waiting with her hands folded, small and grey and efficient, the smell of minerals and antiseptic around her like a halo.

She unwrapped the wound without comment. It had held. She cleaned it anyway, and restitched one place that had pulled, and all the while her hands moved with the precision I remembered from a hundred other times.

"You're different," she said.

"I wounded myself."

"Not that." Her eyes, which never judged, looked at me over her work. "You came to me the first time when you were eight. Hands shaking so badly you couldn't hold the thread. I told you then you would ignore every instruction I ever gave you, and you have, faithfully, for seventeen years." She tied off the stitch. "Today you walked in and you looked at the window."

"The window."

"The narrow one. You have never once looked at it. I have watched you come and go for seventeen years and you have never looked at the windows." She stepped back and began gathering her things. "Whatever is happening to you, it is not the wound. The wound is fine. It is the rest of you I am worried about, and I have no training for that."

I almost told her. I stood in her chamber with the fresh stitches pulling and the words gathered in my mouth — *there is a choir under the training ground made of the voices of people I killed* — and I did not say them. She had fixed me for seventeen years and had never asked for an explanation, and I understood suddenly that if I spoke the words aloud, I would be asking her to carry them, and I had carried enough for both of us and she did not deserve the weight.

"Thank you, Thess," I said.

"Don't thank me. Just don't die. I'm tired of the paperwork." She paused at the door. "And look at the windows more. There is a whole world out there you have not bothered to notice."

That evening, Vark found me on the walkway overlooking the rift-wells, watching a crew of engineers feed a fresh channel of blue light into the deepest shaft.

"You gave them a reason the lords will accept," he said.

"I did."

"But it was not the real reason."

I watched the blue light move through the channel like blood through a vein. "No."

He stood beside me, massive and still, his hands resting on the rail. Below us the wells sang. Between us, the silence of two people who had known each other for seventeen years and had never once said the thing that mattered.

"Whatever you are becoming," Vark said, "it is not Shadowfang."

I turned to look at him.

"I trained Shadowfang," he said. "I know the shape of him. He is a blade. Blades do not question the hand. They do not redraw patrols. They do not teach five hundred killers to listen to the space between heartbeats." His dark eyes held mine. "Whatever you are becoming, I do not know its shape yet."

"A weapon that learned to think," I said.

He had said those words to me the night before, in my quarters, and I had taken them as a promise. Now I said them back, and I felt the difference in my teeth — the way the promise sat in me like a second heartbeat, loud and foreign.

"That was a warning," Vark said.

"I heard it as a promise."

"Then hear this as one." He turned from the rail. "When the lords send you to do the thing they will send you to do next — and they will, soon — remember that you heard the note under the floor. Remember what it is made of." He stepped back into the shadow of the archway. "You are the only one of us who can stand its silence. That makes you the only one who can hear what it is saying."

Then he was gone, and I was alone on the walkway with the wells and the wrong note and the wound in my side pulsing gently, the way a thing pulses when it is trying to tell you it is still there.

I listened to the Choir until the blue light in the shaft dimmed to nothing, and all the while, under the taken voices, under the still chord, under the taste of copper and old rain, the third phrase rose and reached and did not quite catch — and I understood, with a clarity I did not want, that it had never been a phrase at all.

It was a door. And I had spent my whole life standing in front of it with my hand raised, waiting for the dark to blink.

---

## Chapter 19: The Ledger of Names

The summons came before dawn, written in the Auditor's hand on a strip of Void-silver that dissolved in my palm after I read it.

Vaelthorne's message had grown shorter over the years. In the beginning, the Auditor had spoken in paragraphs — elaborate structures of cause and conclusion, each clause a loaded chamber, each sentence a corridor with a door at the end. Then the sentences had thinned. Then the words. Now the Voice arrived as a single instruction, planted behind my eyes before I was fully awake, and I obeyed it the way a blade obeys the angle of the wrist.

*The Unity Market. The beacon. Finalize the mechanism. The ledger is waiting.*

I had planted the beacon in the Unity Market foundations months ago, in the dark under a half-finished tannery, pressing a sliver of compressed Void-silver into the wet clay while the workers slept above me. I had known what it was then. I had called it a listening post. I had told myself that a thing which only listened was not a thing which killed. That was the story I had carried to the roof of the tannery and told the moon while the market's bones went up around me — that I was placing an ear, not a mouth; that the resonance would sing a patient, deadly song only if someone gave it the words.

The Unity Market was the heart of the new peace. That was what the people above me called it — the place where humans and hybrids and elves would trade and mingle and prove that the Accord was more than ink on a treaty. It was where the bright future of the world was being built with hands and beams and banners. And beneath it, in the clay, I had buried the thing that would turn that bright future into a harvest. The surface and the dark, layered like the stone and the roots of a tree. I had learned to see the world that way: never the thing itself, but the thing and the frequency underneath it.

Now the lords had given the frequency its words. They had given it a ledger.

The records office lay in the inner Sanctum, a room of rift-iron shelves and cold light where the Auditor's clerks — masked, silent, fingers moving in the geometric script — maintained the accounts of everything Vaelthorne had ever taken. I had been in that room a hundred times and had never once looked at the ledgers. A weapon does not read the accounts. A weapon does not need to know what it costs. That morning I walked to the central desk and the clerk in charge — a woman I had never seen without her mask — slid a bound volume toward me without looking up.

It was larger than I expected. Bound in something that looked like leather and was not, its pages thin sheets of hammered silver, each one covered in the compact geometric script of the Auditor's accounting.

"Sign here," the clerk said. It was the first time she had spoken. Her voice was flat, practiced, the voice of someone who had said the same words ten thousand times and had stopped hearing them.

"I do not have a name to sign."

"Number then. Designation."

I wrote Shadowfang in the geometric script, and the clerk watched my hand without interest, and when I was done she slid the ledger fully across the desk and went back to whatever work had been occupying her before I came in. I watched her for a moment. Masked, efficient, invisible. She had been maintaining these books for years, entering the names and the frequencies, translating people into notes the machine could hold. Had she ever looked up one of the entries and wondered what had happened to the person in it? I could not tell. The mask gave nothing away. But her hands — small, pale, precise — had a stillness to them that I recognized. They were the hands of someone who had learned not to ask questions whose answers would cost her sleep.

I opened the ledger.

Names.

Not numbers. Not designations. Names. Thousands of them, arranged by district and quarter and street, each one followed by a small notation of frequency — a set of measurements that described the precise note a particular voice made when it said a particular name. The ledger of the Unity Market. Every laborer who had raised a beam, every vendor who would sell beneath a banner, every child who would chase a hoop between the stalls — their names were in this book, and beside each name, the note that would be taken from them when the harvest came due.

I understood then what the Auditor had been building toward with every question in the cave. Truth was justice. The rest was noise. But a name was not noise. A name was the one thing the Choir could not hold without changing it, and the ledger existed to translate each name into a frequency the machine could compress. To finalize a person, the machine first had to know the shape of their song. This book was the shape of ten thousand songs. Ten thousand people who thought they were building a future, and I was holding the account of the song that would be taken from each of them when the future came due.

*This is the first thing you have done with knowledge,* the Voice said. *Every kill before this was ignorance. You were told the target. You were given the frequency. You did not choose the note you cancelled. Now you know what the note is. Now you know whose it is. And still you will do it.*

It was not a question. The Auditor did not ask questions. It laid the fact before me the way a man lays a blade on a table — not to threaten, but to see whether I would pick it up.

I picked it up.

That was the complicity. Not the beacon in the clay. Not the kills in the alley. Those had been done to me, through me, by a hand I could not see and had spent seventeen years trying to identify. This was different. This was the first act I performed with full knowledge of what it was and whom it would cost. I closed the ledger, and I carried it out of the records office, and I walked toward the Unity Market with the weight of ten thousand names in my hands, and I did not set it down.

The market was nearly finished.

I arrived by the north road in the grey light before the workers, and I stood on the rise where I had stood a hundred times in the months since the beacon went into the clay, and I looked down at the thing the lords called a harvest mechanism and everyone else called a home.

The banners were up now — indigo and silver, the colours of the Accord, snapping in the morning wind. The central hub had its roof. The stalls had their awnings. Someone had planted a row of quick-growing herbs in a trough beside the tannery, and someone else had built a small wooden shelf for them, the kind of shelf a person builds when they intend to be somewhere for a long time. The scaffolding was coming down. In three days the gates would open and ten thousand people would pour through them to buy and sell and laugh and touch, and beneath their feet the shard in the clay would be waiting with its patient, deadly song.

And now it would have names to sing.

The workers began to arrive at dawn. Humans first, in clusters of three and four, carrying tool bundles and lunch pails, their boots leaving dark prints in the wet clay. Then the hybrids, moving alone or in pairs, their anatomies leaving distinct tracks — wolf-kin prints heavy and wide, raven-kin prints thin and sharp, the long double-stride of a fox-kin woman who moved like smoke. The earth bore the record of their coming like a ledger, each entry clear and legible, and I stood above it with my own ledger open in my hands and felt the two accounts line up.

A human woman handed a hammer to a wolf-kin laborer, their fingers brushing in the exchange. A group of adolescent hybrids laughed at something a human teenager had said, the sound carrying up the rise to where I stood. An old man, human, leaned on his shovel while a raven-kin woman showed him a trick for sharpening a blade, her long fingers moving patiently in the air.

A child came out of the tannery with a length of offcut wood in his hands, beating it against his leg in an irregular rhythm as he walked. He was human, eight or nine, with a gap where a tooth had been and a concentration on his face that belonged to a boy doing something important. He stopped at the herb shelf, examined the empty trough with the seriousness of a magistrate, then climbed onto a crate and sat swinging his legs, still beating the stick against his leg. The rhythm was wrong — too fast in one place, dragging in another — and I found myself wanting to reach out and correct it, to tap the beat into the clay the way I had once tapped the fork against the Null-Quartz. I had been trained to find the beat in everything and make it true. Now I understood that the boy's wrong rhythm was the truest thing in the market, because it was his, and it changed every time he struck the wood. The ledger in my hands had an entry for him. I knew it without looking. I had read it an hour ago.

These were the same people I had watched that afternoon months ago, the same handshake, the same smile, the same laughter crossing the species line. Nothing about them had changed. The only thing that had changed was me. I now knew the note each of them made when they said their own name, and I was holding the book that would hand those notes to the Choir. This was what the peace looked like from below: not a treaty, not a speech, not a crown or a marriage or a hall full of voting lords, but a hammer passed between two hands and a boy beating a stick against his leg. The bright arc of the world, and I was its shadow, measuring it for the machine.

Somewhere in that bright arc, my brother stood. I felt him the way I felt the market's resonance — as a pressure at the edge of my hearing. The girl with the light, the one who had looked at me in the throne room like she could see who I was before all this, was with him. The Auditor's threads had brought me fragments: a shared laugh, a hand held too long, the way his frequency had changed since Aethoria — warmer, steadier, less guarded. Lukas was being loved. Lukas was letting himself be loved. And I was beneath the market that celebrated the world he was helping to build, holding the ledger that would take its song.

*Lukas is happy,* the Voice said.

I did not react. I had learned, in the years of the Auditor's shaping, to receive its statements without giving them the dignity of a response. But this one found a place in me the others could not reach.

*He has been made welcome in the Dawn Court,* the Voice continued. *He stands in the light now. He is celebrated. The girl who carries the generative frequency looks at him with the expression the Auditor's models call devotion. He is thought of as a hero. He is given the things you were told you were made to protect.*

The old man below straightened and wiped his brow with a rag, and the raven-kin woman said something to him that made him throw his head back and laugh, and the laugh came up the rise to me, warm and unguarded.

*He is happy because you were discarded,* the Voice said. *The instrument was placed in the dark so that the subject could stand in the light. This was the design from the beginning. You were never coming home to him, Shadowfang. You were being removed from him so that he could shine.*

There was a moment — a real moment, a physical event in my chest — in which something in me reached for the old warmth. The biological satisfaction of a body that had been asked to do something and had done it. The release. The sharp, content certainty that had followed every kill and made the following of them possible. It rose in me now like an old appetite waking, and I understood with a horrible clarity that the Auditor had built this moment too — that it had known the way to make me compliant was not pain but this: the promise that if I fed the machine enough, I would become the darkness that made my brother's light possible. A purpose. A reason. A way to be useful even in exile.

I wanted it. That was the horror. I wanted to believe that I had been discarded for a reason, that my seventeen years in the dark had bought Lukas the light he stood in, that there was arithmetic to the suffering and I was on the giving end of it. It would have been so easy. It would have felt so warm.

I closed the ledger.

"No," I said. It was the first time I had spoken a denial to the Voice aloud, and the word came out rough, scraped raw by disuse. "He is not happy because I was discarded."

*The statement is not an emotional preference. It is a fact.*

"He is happy because he is a good man standing in a place where good men can be happy." My voice was quiet. Below me, the old man had gone back to work, and the raven-kin woman had returned to her sharpening, and the laughter had dissolved into the ordinary music of a hundred tools striking a hundred different materials. "You cannot take that away from him and give it to me. You do not have it to give."

The Voice went silent. Not absent — silent. The difference was enormous. Absence was a hole; silence was a held breath. Something in the machine had stopped to listen.

*Proceed,* it said at last.

I proceeded.

I went down to the foundation at midday, when the sun was high and the workers were eating, and I opened the seam in the clay that I had sealed months before. The shard was where I had left it, a sliver of compressed Void-silver no larger than a finger, its single facet catching the light in a way that made my eye slide past it, unwilling to focus. It was warm. It had been singing since the moment I planted it — patient, deadly, gathering the resonance of every footstep that crossed above it, every voice that spoke above it, every note the market made as it came together. Now it was hungry for names.

The ledger lay open on the clay beside it. The first name on the page was a woman named Ildra, a stonemason, her frequency noted in the small geometric script — a low, steady note with a bright edge where she laughed. I had watched her work. I had seen her hands. They were cracked and stained and sure.

To finalize a name, the machine required only that the frequency be entered into the resonance and confirmed. The shard would do the rest — would find that note in the living woman above me and draw it down, compress it, add it to the still chord in the chamber beneath the training ground. It took less than a breath. It always took less than a breath.

I pressed my thumb to the shard and spoke the name.

Ildra's note rose out of the clay like a thread drawn from water — low, steady, bright at the edge where she laughed — and the shard took it, and the seam in the clay closed over the taking, and somewhere above me a stonemason set down her hammer and reached for a chisel and did not know that a small portion of the song she made in the world had just been moved somewhere she would never find it.

Then I came to the second name, and I did not speak it.

The ledger had been designed by the Auditor, and the Auditor had designed it well. The names were entered in the order the harvest would take them — the most resonant first, the notes that would feed the Choir most efficiently. Ildra was the first. The second was a child. A boy, eleven years old, whose frequency was noted as high and clear with a tremor in it that the clerk who had written the entry had not thought to hide. I had seen him that morning, chasing a hoop between the stalls, his laughter carrying up the rise with the others.

I held my thumb above the shard and I did not press it down.

*Proceed,* the Voice said.

I looked at the boy's name. It was a name I will not write here. Some names are not mine to carry, even in the dark. I looked at it, and I thought of a child in a wheat field running with his arms wide, mouth open, the uncomplicated joy of a creature who had not yet learned what the world kept in its ledgers, and I understood that Lukas had run like that once and I had loved him for it and I was now holding the note that would stop another boy from ever running like that again.

I pressed my thumb to the shard.

But I did not speak the name. Instead, I did the thing I had spent the last hour teaching myself to do in the quiet of the records office, while the clerk sat silent behind her mask and the Auditor's clerks moved through their accounts. I altered the frequency.

The ledger's notations were not fixed. They were written in the Auditor's script, which was a script of measurement — and measurement, I had learned in seventeen years of being measured, was a thing that could be changed. A note described was a note controlled. If I shifted the boy's frequency by a fraction — a fraction too small for the clerks to notice in their nightly audit, too small for the shard to register as a deviation — then when the harvest came, the machine would find a note that did not match the boy. It would pass him by. Not save him. The Choir would take someone else in his place, because the machine always took its due. But it would not take him.

I shifted the frequency by a fraction and turned the page.

The third name was a baker, a woman whose frequency was low and warm with a small catch in it that the clerk had noted as "frequent laughter." I bent her note by a fraction. The fourth was a fox-kin laborer with a young daughter — the entry had a notation in the margin, added by whoever compiled the ledger, that read *bond: child, high resonance*, as if a mother's love were a variable to be measured. I bent that note too, and felt something in my chest tighten at the small cruelty of the notation. The fifth was the old man who had been sharpening a blade an hour ago. I held my thumb over his name for a long moment, remembering the way he had thrown his head back and laughed, and I bent his note further than I had bent the others — a full half-step, dangerous, the kind of deviation that would show in the audit if I did not cover it — and then I covered it by shifting the two entries after it in the opposite direction, balancing the account the way a clerk balances a column of numbers. The Auditor had taught me to balance accounts. I had never thought to use the lesson against it.

By the time the workers came back from their meal, I had altered eleven entries, and blood had started to run from my nose. I wiped it on my sleeve and kept working. Each alteration cost me something — not the warm satisfaction, which stayed away, which I kept away, but a small, sharp effort of attention, the way a man holds a breath he cannot afford to release. The Voice grew colder with each one, and hotter, too, a strange combination I had no name for — the pressure of a signal straining to reach me through a frequency that was slowly pulling apart.

Anomaly, it said. Then, again: Anomaly. Anomaly. Anomaly.

By the twentieth entry the word had stopped being a note in a ledger and had started being a warning.

I did not stop. I worked through the afternoon, hidden in the seam beneath the tannery, while the market came together above me and the workers raised their beams and their banners and their small wooden shelves for the herbs they intended to tend for a long time. I altered thirty-one names. I took one — Ildra — and I could not take another, and the machine went on singing with one less note than it had been promised and thirty-one notes that would not answer when it called.

When the sun began to go down, I closed the ledger and sealed the seam and climbed out of the foundation into the cooling air. The market was quiet now. The workers had gone. The banners snapped in the wind, indigo and silver, and the herb shelf stood empty beside the tannery, waiting for morning hands.

I stood on the rise and held the ledger and listened. Below me, the boy with the gap in his teeth climbed down from the crate and ran to catch up with a group of older children, still beating the stick against his leg, his wrong rhythm carrying across the market and fading into the general music of tools and voices and footsteps. He did not know his name had been in a book. He did not know a hand had reached into the clay beneath his feet and turned his note slightly out of true so that a machine would pass him by. He would go on being findable by his mother, by his friends, by whoever would eventually teach him to beat the rhythm right — and then, perhaps, to beat it wrong again on purpose, the way the living do. That was the whole of what I had bought with my afternoon. Not a life saved. A song left where it belonged.

The walk back to Vaelthorne took an hour. My side had opened under the exertion of the clay and the bending, and the bandage Thess had placed that morning was stiff with blood, and the nosebleed had not fully stopped. I walked with my collar up against the cold and the ledger pressed under my arm, and every few paces I reached up to touch the wound and came away with red on my fingers. The city rose ahead of me, black and functional and indifferent, and I thought about the clerk in the records office with her pale, still hands, and I wondered whether, tonight, when she entered the thirty-one discrepancies into the nightly audit, she would pause over the boy's name and wonder why it looked slightly different than it had yesterday. I hoped she would not. I hoped she would go home to a table set for four, if such a table existed for her, and never know that a man had bent a column of numbers to keep a child's song in the world.

Under the market, the shard sang. It was a thinner song than it had been that morning — missing a note here and there, stumbling over frequencies that did not quite match, the patient deadly song developing a hitch I had put there. It would not be enough. I knew that. Thirty-one names out of ten thousand was a rounding error in the Auditor's accounting. The harvest would take its due and more to make up the difference. But somewhere in the market, thirty-one people would go on being findable by the people who loved them, and a stonemason named Ildra would not, and the arithmetic of that was the only arithmetic I had ever done that I could stand to look at.

The Voice spoke once more as I walked back toward Vaelthorne in the dark.

*You have altered the ledger,* it said. It was not a question. It was a statement of fact, delivered with the flat, terrible certainty of a machine that had finished measuring something and found it wanting. *The deviation will be corrected.*

"I know," I said.

*You do not know what corrects deviation.*

I walked on. The wound in my side pulsed with my heartbeat, and the taste of copper and old rain gathered in my mouth, and under everything — under the market, under the city, under the rift-wells and the Choir and the cold geometry of the Auditor's accounting — the third phrase rose and reached and did not quite catch, the way it always had, the way it always would, until somebody finally taught me how to hold a note that wanted to leave.

---

## Chapter 20: The Fractured Frequency

They came for me at the third watch, three nights after the ledger.

I felt them before I saw them — not as footsteps, for they made none, but as a change in the temperature of the corridor outside my quarters. The air went still in the way it goes still when a door has opened somewhere and the space beyond it is colder than the space you occupy. I woke with my hand already on the knife beneath my pillow, which was not reflex but the residue of seventeen years of being taught that sleep was a vulnerability and vulnerability was an insult.

The corridor held four figures.

They wore no armor. That was the first strangeness. Vaelthorne's guards were iron and shadow and insignia, but these wore long coats of a grey so neutral it seemed to drain the light from around them, and their faces were covered by smooth masks with no slits at all — not iron, not Null-Quartz, but something softer, a pale ceramic that reflected nothing and revealed nothing. They stood in the corridor with their hands hanging at their sides and their heads slightly inclined, as if listening to something I could not hear.

Still Ones. I had heard of them but never seen them. The Auditor's correction-enforcers — the things it sent when an instrument developed a deviation it could not correct from a distance.

I had heard the stories in the Pits, told in the dark between sessions by boys who had seen a Still One take someone. They did not kill, the stories said. Killing was a frequency, and the Still Ones were sent to correct frequencies, not to end them. They reached into a man's chest — into the place where his song lived — and they compressed it. What came out was a person who still breathed and still walked and still answered to a name, but who no longer made the small sounds that proved he was in there. The other boys had called them the emptied. I had listened to those stories with the detached interest of a child who had not yet understood that they were descriptions of his own future. Now four of them stood in my corridor, and I was the deviation they had come to correct, and I understood with a cold, precise clarity that running was the only option that was not surrender.

The nearest one turned its masked face toward my door. It had no eyes that I could see, but I felt the attention land on me like a physical weight, and every instinct I had ever been given screamed at me to run.

I did not run. I had learned, in the Null-Quartz chamber, that the correct response to terror was to make the body still and let the mind catalogue. So I catalogued. Four of them. No weapons visible. No Discord signature — that was the second strangeness, and the more terrible one. They carried no frequency of their own. They were not compressed into weapons the way the Vanguard were. They were something else. Empty vessels, moving with a terrible, fluid quiet, the way water moves downhill.

The nearest one spoke. Its voice came from everywhere and nowhere, the same impossible ventriloquism as the Auditor's — but flatter, emptier, as if the words were being read by something that had learned speech from a dead language.

"Fenris," it said.

Not Shadowfang. Not a designation. My given name, spoken in a voice with no mouth behind it.

My blood went cold. In seventeen years, no one inside Vaelthorne had called me Fenris. The name had been taken from me the way the Pits took names — systematically, without cruelty, as a matter of procedure. For the machine to know it, the machine had to have reached back into a place I had thought was sealed.

"I am here to correct a deviation," the Still One said. "You altered thirty-one entries in the Unity Market harvest ledger. You redirected five Vanguard patrols away from civilian zones. You taught the formation to listen to unauthorized frequencies." The mask inclined. "You are carrying an unauthorized frequency yourself."

The Voice stirred in my skull — a pulse of cold recognition, the way a man's pulse jumps when he sees something he half expected. It did not speak. It *recognized*. And in that instant, in the space between the Still One's flat words and the Voice's wordless pulse, I heard something I had never heard before.

They were the same frequency.

The Voice in my head and the Still One in the corridor — they were not two things communicating. They were one thing, two mouths of the same instrument. The certainty I had carried since I was eight years old, the cold companion that had guided every kill and every mission and told me truth was justice and the rest was noise, was not a voice in my mind. It was a frequency installed in my mind. A signal, transmitted from the Auditor through the threads of the Discord, received by a receiver that had been built into me in the Null-Quartz chamber when I was eight years old and screaming and did not know that the thing being done to me had a name.

I had thought it was my own certainty. It had never been mine.

Seventeen years of a voice in my skull, and I had never once asked where it came from. That was the thing that stopped me — not the revelation, but the fact of my own blindness. The Auditor had told me truth was justice and the rest was noise, and I had believed it with the whole of me, and the believing had felt like the most private thing in the world, like the one part of me the conditioning had not touched. It had been the opposite. It had been the conditioning. It had been the deepest layer of it — not the pain, not the fork, not the Null-Quartz chamber, but this: a frequency planted so young and so deep that I had mistaken it for the shape of my own mind. Every certainty I had ever felt had been borrowed. Every moment I had thought *I know this* had been the Auditor speaking with my mouth.

And yet the guilt had been mine. The guilt, the doubt, the small stubborn song — those had not come from the machine. They had come from the place the conditioning had never reached, the place where my mother's lullaby lived, where Lukas's face lived, where the taste of copper and old rain had waited seventeen years to be noticed. The machine had built the certainty. It had not built the thing that doubted it. That was mine. That was the only thing in me that had never been installed, and it was the thing the Still Ones had come to remove.

The knowledge arrived without drama. It did not shatter me. It simply re-arranged everything I had ever felt into a new shape, as if I had spent seventeen years looking at a painting and someone had turned it over to show the wiring and the nails behind it. The warmth I had mistaken for purpose. The satisfaction I had mistaken for morality. The certainty I had mistaken for myself. All of it was a signal, injected, tuned, maintained. I was a receiver that had learned to think it was the broadcast.

"You are carrying an unauthorized frequency," the Still One repeated, and this time the words carried a different weight — not an accusation, but a diagnostic. It was telling me what it had found. It was telling me what it was going to remove.

"No," I said.

It came out before I could stop it, the same way the word had come out in the cave — *the Auditor doesn't account for guilt* — rising from a place the conditioning had never reached. I did not know what I was refusing. I only knew that I was refusing. Then I did the thing I had been practicing in the dark for three nights, since the moment in the Null-Quartz chamber when I had heard the third phrase held in the still chord and understood it was a door.

I detuned.

It was not a physical act. There was no button to press, no muscle to flex. It was an act of attention — a deliberate, painful reorientation of the way I listened to myself. For seventeen years I had listened to the Voice with the whole of my attention, had let it fill the space behind my eyes until it was indistinguishable from thought. Now I reached for the space *around* it. The margin. The place where the signal ended and something else began.

And there, under the Auditor's frequency, I heard the world.

It was like standing in a room with a lamp lit and discovering, suddenly, that the room had windows. The Auditor's frequency had been the lamp — bright, cold, filling the space behind my eyes so completely that I had thought it was the whole of the light. Now I saw the edges of it. I saw where it ended. And beyond the edges, the world had been singing all along, a vast, untidy chorus that the lamp had made invisible.

It came in fragments at first. The rift-well hum beneath the city, but not as I had ever heard it — not as a single note but as a chord made of a thousand small voices, the engineers and the guards and the prisoners who lived in the shadows of the wells, each one humming their own note without knowing it. The sound of the city breathing: the bakery sliding loaves onto a sill two streets over, a woman laughing in a window, a child crying and then stopping, the wet slap of a guard's boot on stone. These were not sounds I did not normally hear. I heard them always. But I had been trained to hear them as data — inputs to be filtered, deviations to be catalogued, noise to be discarded. Now I heard them as music. A thousand separate notes, each one belonging to someone, each one a small, stubborn song that the machine had never managed to fully compress.

And under that, fainter than everything, a melody I knew.

My mother's lullaby. The tune I had carried in the place where the conditioning could not dig. But it was different now — not the two lines I had been able to remember, not the memory of a song, but the song itself, as if the world were humming it through the stone and the air and the cold corridors of Vaelthorne. *Hush now, little shadow, the moon's keeping score. Close your eyes, little brother, I'll find you once more.* And then — reaching, rising, the third phrase, the one she had never caught, the one that had waited in the still chord of the Choir like a bird in a closed hand —

I did not catch it. But the detuning brought me closer. The frequency of the world was a frequency that changed, that moved, that made itself up as it went, and the third phrase belonged to that frequency — the one thing the machine could not hold, because to hold it would be to change it, and to change it would be to admit that the thing it was holding was not the thing it had been given.

The Still Ones moved toward me.

They did not hurry. They did not need to. Four figures flowing down the corridor with that terrible fluid quiet, and I stood in my doorway with my hand on a knife that would do no good against creatures who carried no frequency to cut. The Voice — the signal — flared in my skull, a sudden cold surge, and I felt the detuning slip. The world's music faded. The Auditor's certainty rushed back in like water into an empty vessel.

*Correct the deviation,* it said. *Return to compliance. The instrument does not choose.*

Then the nearest Still One was in front of me, and its hand — pale, gloved, impossibly gentle — was on my chest, over the place where the Discord lived, over the place where my blood met my bones. It was searching for the unauthorized frequency. It was going to reach in and take the small, stubborn song I had just heard and compress it back into silence.

I did the only thing I could think of. I opened my mouth and I sang.

Not loudly. Not well. The voice that came out of me was rough and unused, a man's voice singing a child's lullaby in a cold corridor at the third watch of the night. But it was a frequency that changed. It moved. It made itself up as it went, because I could not remember the third phrase and so I invented one — a clumsy, reaching thing that rose too high and came down wrong and then rose again, different, refusing to settle into the shape the machine expected.

The Still One's hand stopped.

Its head inclined. The pale mask turned toward my mouth as if the thing inside it were listening to something for the first time and did not have the vocabulary for it. The other three paused behind it. And in the space of that pause — that single, impossible pause — I felt the signal in my skull falter. Not break. Falter. It was a frequency, and frequencies could be disrupted, and I had just introduced into the room a sound that refused to hold still long enough to be corrected.

I did not wait to see what it would do next. I pulled away from its hand, slammed the door, and ran.

I ran through the Sanctum the way I had never run in seventeen years — not with the silent precision of the assassin but with the clumsy, desperate speed of a man fleeing a thing he cannot fight. Behind me, the Still Ones flowed through the door without breaking stride. They did not make a sound. That was what woke the city — not noise, but the absence of it, the way a room goes quiet when a predator enters. Doors opened along the corridor. Faces appeared in the gaps. None of them spoke. In Vaelthorne, you learned not to notice things. It was a survival skill.

I took the lower passages, the old ones, the ones Vark had taught me before the conditioning had narrowed my world to targets and routes. I ran through the apothecary on Grief Row — knocked over a jar of something that smelled of burnt sugar, left a trail of dark liquid on the stone — and out through a maintenance seam that opened onto the outer ward. The cold air hit my face like a slap. I kept running.

I ran until the wound in my side opened and the blood ran warm down my leg and my breath came in ragged, animal gasps, and the city was behind me and the Greywood was ahead and the Sound was gone from my skull — gone, not silent, gone, as if the detuning had shaken it loose and it had fallen out of me like a dead thing.

The absence was terrifying.

I had not known how much of my thinking had been done by the Voice. Without it, the world rushed in at full volume — every sound a separate thing demanding to be heard, every object a weight in my hands, every memory a live wire. I stood in the dark on the edge of the Greywood with blood running down my leg and my hands shaking, and I felt naked in a way I had never felt in all my years of being stripped and shaped and catalogued. The conditioning had been a skin. I had not known it was a skin until it was gone. And underneath it was a man I did not recognize — a man who was afraid, who was bleeding, who had just sung a lullaby to a creature with no face and had meant every word.

I kept the detuning. I did not know how to turn it back on. I suspected, with a cold certainty that did not come from the Auditor, that I did not want to.

The waystation stood where the Greywood thinned into the borderlands, a squat stone building with a slate roof and a lantern burning above the door. It was a Dawn Court post — one of the few places between Vaelthorne and the civilized lands where a man could sit down without being measured. It was also a nest of the new politics: couriers moving between the Accord's capitals, informers trading in the small coin of who had said what to whom, spies wearing the faces of merchants and pilgrims. I knew it because I had passed it a dozen times on missions and had never once gone in. Tonight I went in.

The common room was empty save for one figure at a corner table.

Nyssa did not look up when I entered. She was dressed in the travel clothes I remembered — practical, grey, the colours of a woman who expected to be somewhere else by morning — and her amber eyes were on a folded map spread across the table. A cup of something steamed at her elbow. She had the look she always had, the look of someone who had been expecting you and had decided not to make anything of it. In Aethoria she had been Lukas's eyes — a spy in the service of the political arc, collecting facts the way other people collected coins. Here, in the borderlands, she moved between both worlds, and I had never been certain which side she reported to on any given day. That was what made her dangerous. That was what made her useful.

"You're bleeding," she said.

"Everyone keeps telling me."

"It's a gift. I collect facts the way other people collect coins, and this one is free." She folded the map slowly, deliberately, and put it aside. "Three facts, then. One: the Vanguard patrols in Vaelthorne's low quarters went quiet two nights ago. Two: the Unity Market harvest ledger was altered in thirty-one places last night — a small enough deviation that the clerks' audit will not catch it for a week. Three: four Still Ones were dispatched from the inner Sanctum at the third watch and are at this moment hunting a man who is no longer in his quarters." She looked up then, and those amber eyes fixed on me with the intensity that was simply thoroughness. "I am assuming the man is you."

I stood in the doorway with blood on my leg and the cold night at my back, and I did not answer.

"I am not going to ask you why," Nyssa said. "I have learned that asking you why produces answers designed for the Auditor, and I have no interest in the Auditor's answers." She pushed the cup across the table toward the empty chair opposite her. "Sit. The tea is real. It is the last real thing this side of the border."

I sat. The tea was hot and tasted of leaves and smoke and something floral I could not name. It was the first thing I had tasted in years that was not iron and ash and the mineral bitterness of fuel. I drank it and felt the warmth of it go down my throat and settle in my stomach, and I understood that I had been cold for a very long time and had not known it.

Nyssa watched me drink. She did not speak. That was her gift — the space she left around a thing, the way she let a silence sit until it was ready to give up its fact. It was the opposite of the Auditor, which filled every silence with certainty. Nyssa let the silence mean what it wanted.

"You are carrying something that isn't in the Auditor's calculations," she said at last. "I saw it in Aethoria. I see it now. Guilt makes instruments unreliable, and you are so full of guilt you are vibrating with it." She tilted her head. "But that is not what changed tonight. Tonight something else happened to you. Your eyes are different."

"My eyes."

"You are looking at the room," she said. "Not through it. The Shadowfang I knew in Aethoria looked through everything — through the room, through the people, through the walls, at the target behind. He never saw the room. Tonight you walked in and you saw the room. The cracked window. The stain on the floor. The way the lantern throws the tree's shadow on the glass." She paused. "Who did that to you?"

"No one," I said. "I did it to myself."

"People do not do this to themselves." She said it without emphasis, the way she said everything — a fact placed on the table like a coin. "Something happened to you tonight. The Still Ones were sent, you ran, and something broke in you on the way. I can see the shape of the break but not the cause." She tilted her head. "The Auditor's frequency — did you hear it differently tonight?"

I looked at her. Nyssa had no way of knowing that. No one knew that the Voice was a frequency, not a voice — the Auditor's own clerks did not know, I suspected; the lords certainly did not. And yet she had asked the question as casually as she might have asked about the weather.

"You are a dangerous woman, Nyssa," I said.

"I am a curious one. Dangerous is a conclusion you have drawn, and I have found that men like you draw it about women like me when they would prefer not to answer a question." She did not press. That was the thing about her — she never pressed. She placed the fact on the table and left it there and let you decide whether to pick it up. "I will ask once more, and then I will spend the question elsewhere. Did you hear it differently?"

"Yes," I said. "It is not in my head. It was put there. And tonight I heard it from the outside."

She nodded once, slowly, as if I had confirmed something she had already known and had only wanted to hear me say. "Then you are no longer an instrument," she said. "You are a man with a choice. That is a far more dangerous thing to be."

"A dangerous man with excellent taste, then." She reached into her sleeve and drew out a folded strip of paper, which she placed on the table between us with two fingers, as if it were a thing that might bite. "A fact. Free of charge. The ledger of the Unity Market has a companion volume in the inner Sanctum — the harvest schedule. It lists every finalized zone and every beacon due to come online before the threshold. I have been trying to get eyes on it for six months."

She slid the paper across the table. I did not pick it up.

"Why give it to me?" I asked.

"Because you are going to go looking for it anyway," Nyssa said. "And because when you do, you will find things in it that you were not meant to find — graves, and names, and the arithmetic of what your machine has eaten." Her amber eyes held mine. "I do not ask why you want it. I do not interrogate. I collect facts and I spend them when the exchange rate is favourable. This one is not charity. I am buying something with it."

"What?"

"Your honesty, eventually." She stood, pulling her coat tight. "You have never once told me the truth about what you are doing. Not in Aethoria, not in the market, not now. But you are coming apart, Fenris. People who are coming apart have less use for the stories that held them together." She moved toward the door, then stopped. "The companion ledger is in the chamber below the records office. Not the one you used. The one below it. The Auditor keeps it close."

Then she was gone, and the common room held only the smell of tea and smoke and the faint warmth of a presence that had been there and had chosen not to stay. I understood what she was doing. She was a spy in a world of spies, and suspicion was the air she breathed — if the Auditor ever learned who had given me the ledger's location, Nyssa would be questioned, and the questions would be the kind that end careers or lives. She had chosen to be seen giving it to me anyway. That was either a miscalculation or the boldest gamble I had ever witnessed, and I could not tell which.

I sat for a long time. The paper lay on the table between my hands. I did not pick it up for a while — not until the tea had gone cold and the lantern above the door had guttered low — and when I finally did, I unfolded it and found not a map but a list: nine place-names, written in Nyssa's small, precise hand, each one followed by a single word.

*Finalized.*

I folded it again and put it in my pocket, beside the cold Oakhaven shard. Then I walked out into the borderland night with the Sound gone from my skull and the world's music pouring in through every crack in me — the wind in the dead grass, the creak of the waystation sign, the distant cry of a bird that had no business being awake at this hour — and for the first time in seventeen years, I listened to the world and did not reach for a blade.

Far behind me, in the deep places beneath Vaelthorne, the Still Ones flowed through the corridors like water looking for a crack, and the Choir went on singing with thirty-one notes missing from its song.

---

## Chapter 21: The Unmaking of Shadowfang

Vark was waiting for me in the forge.

I knew he would be. There were only a handful of places in Vaelthorne where a man could go to be alone with a thing that needed breaking, and the Null-Quartz forge was the one Vark had used since I was eight years old. It lay below the training grounds, adjacent to the chamber where the Choir sang, and its walls were lined with the same sound-drinking stone so that the noise of the work would not carry. A forge is a place of noise — hammer on metal, bellows, the scream of heated iron quenched in water — and Vaelthorne had no tolerance for noise it had not authorized.

I went to him because there was nowhere else to go. The Still Ones hunted the corridors. The lords would have me arrested on sight. And Vark — Vark was the only person in the city who would meet me with a conversation before he met me with a blade.

He stood at the anvil when I entered, his great frame bent over a bar of rift-iron glowing dull red. He did not look up. The hammer in his hand came down, and the iron flattened, and the sound died instantly against the Null-Quartz walls as if it had never been made.

The forge was hot enough to make the air shimmer, and the heat carried the smell of iron and oil and old sweat — the smell of my childhood, of the man who had shaped me standing over this same anvil while I held the Discord in a chest that had not yet learned what it was for. The walls of Null-Quartz drank the light as well as the sound, so the room seemed to end a few feet from where I stood, a small island of forge-fire surrounded by absolute dark. The Choir pressed at the edges of that dark, a faint chord only I could hear, and it occurred to me that Vark had been working in this room for seventeen years with a machine singing three chambers away and had never once known it was there.

"You're bleeding through your bandages," he said.

"I noticed."

"Sit down."

"I did not come to sit."

He set the hammer on the anvil and finally turned to face me. The forge light carved deep lines into his weathered face — the face of a man who had spent forty years being certain and had never once questioned the cost of it. His dark eyes found mine and held. Behind him, the bar of iron cooled from red to black, and in the silence the Choir's note pressed against the room from the chamber next door.

"You were visited last night," Vark said. "Four Still Ones. They went to your quarters and found an empty room." He paused. "You ran."

"I ran."

"Shadowfang does not run."

"Shadowfang is not here."

That landed. I saw it land — a small tightening around his eyes, the way a man's face changes when someone has said the name of a thing he had been trying not to see. He picked up the hammer again, turned back to the iron, and brought it down. The sound died. He did that three times before he spoke.

"When you were eight," he said, "I put you in this forge for the first time. Do you remember?"

"I remember."

"You could not hold the Discord then. It tore out of you every time the fork struck, and the walls drank it, and you screamed until you had no voice left." The hammer came down. "I told you then that the pain was the price of becoming something useful. You believed me. That was the shape of you — a child who believed pain was a price and usefulness was a thing worth paying for." He turned. "Is that still the shape of you now?"

I walked to the anvil and stood across it from him, the glowing iron between us.

"No," I said.

"Then what is the shape of you now?"

I did not answer with words. I reached for the Discord — not the way I had been taught, not the cold, focused compression that gathered it into a blade behind my sternum, but the way I had learned to reach for it in the three days since the detuning. I let it come as sound. A note. The small, stubborn note of a man standing in a forge who had been told he was a weapon and had decided he was something else. It rose in me without warmth. That was the change. The biological satisfaction did not come. The old appetite stayed quiet. I held the Discord like a man holds a cup of water he intends to drink from, not spill.

Vark's eyes widened a fraction. He felt it — the difference in the frequency. He had trained me to compress the Discord into violence, and I was now holding it as something else: a note, a song, a thing that changed as I held it.

"That is not how you use it," he said.

"No."

"It is not how I taught you."

"No."

"Then show me what it is."

He did not ask. Vark never asked. He made a statement that was a challenge, and then he made the first move.

He came across the anvil with the hammer in his hand — not the small forging hammer but a war-hammer he had kept hanging on the wall, a thing of rift-iron and compressed Void with a head the size of my skull. He moved with a speed that belied his bulk, forty years of combat compressed into a body that had never once doubted where it was going or why. The hammer came down at my shoulder, and the air before it screamed.

I had fought him a hundred times in the training ring. I knew the shape of him the way a man knows the shape of a door he has walked through every day of his life — knew that the first blow was never the real blow, that the war-hammer would come down hard and then come back across in a shorter arc that caught men on the rebound, knew that his left foot would pivot a half-beat before the strike and that the pivot was the only warning you got. Seventeen years of that knowledge sat in my muscles, and my body began to move before my mind had decided what to do — began to step in the pattern Vark had drilled into me, the pattern of a weapon meeting a weapon.

I broke the pattern.

I did not block it. Blocking was the old way — Discord against force, compression against compression, the blade meeting the blade. I stepped inside the arc of the swing instead, the way a man steps inside a wave, and I let the hammer pass where my shoulder had been, and I put my hand — open, flat — against the center of his chest.

And I made him hear.

Not with force. Not by driving the Discord into him the way a blade drives into flesh. I opened the note in me and let it pour into the space between us, the small, stubborn song of a man who had been a weapon and was now something else, and I shaped it into the one thing Vark had never learned to hear: the world beneath the machine.

He stopped.

It was not a physical stop — his momentum carried him two paces past me, the hammer's head striking the Null-Quartz wall and staggering him with the recoil — but something in him stopped. The certainty. The absolute, unexamined certainty that had carried him across forty years of battlefields and training grounds and had never once required him to ask whether the thing he was doing was right. I felt it the way I felt the Choir — as a frequency, a tight, hard note held behind his sternum, the signature of a man compressed into a weapon and told it was a weapon and nothing more. And under my hand, in the space of a heartbeat, I made that note falter.

He heard it. I knew he heard it because his face changed — not with pain, not with fear, but with something worse: recognition. The way a man's face changes when he has spent his whole life looking at a wall and someone has suddenly turned him around to see what the wall was hiding. His eyes, fixed on mine, lost their flat, assessing hardness. Something like wonder came into them. Then something like grief.

The hammer fell from his hand. It hit the floor with a sound the walls drank, and he stood in the middle of the forge with his chest heaving and his hands half-raised, as if he had just been given a thing he did not know how to hold.

"What—" he said. His voice was not the deep resonant voice I knew. It was thinner. "What is that?"

"It is the world," I said. "The part you were never taught to hear."

He staggered to the anvil and put both hands on it, his great frame folding slightly, the way a tree folds in a wind it has never met before. For a long moment he said nothing. The forge ticked and cooled around us. The Choir's note pressed at the edges of my hearing, and under it — for the first time with another person, with a living witness — I heard the world's music move.

Then Vark did something I had never seen him do. He wept.
