The carvers had known something. They had known that a sound, once made, did not belong to the throat that made it.

I stood in the ruin and put my hand on the wolf's open mouth. The stone was cold. The carved howl ran under my fingers like a groove in a record, and I understood that the men who had cut it had been trying to do what I was now trying to do: leave a sound behind that would outlive the body.

The Voice stirred.

*This is not a tactical location. Depart.*

"I'm testing something."

*Testing requires a hypothesis. State the hypothesis.*

I thought about it. The child in the market had asked me *how*, and I had told her I would sing back, and Vark had asked me what I was choosing, and I had told him the howling. Neither answer had been a hypothesis. Both had been a decision dressed as an explanation. The machine wanted a hypothesis because a hypothesis could be measured, and a thing that could be measured could be filed, and a thing that could be filed could be corrected. I had spent seventeen years giving the machine things it could file.

This time I would not.

"The hypothesis," I said, "is that you are wrong."

*Elaborate.*

"You think a voice is a thing that belongs to a throat. You think when the throat stops, the voice stops, and the frequency goes back into the silence, and the ledger balances. You've built a hundred years of machinery on that belief — the Pits, the forks, the Null-Quartz, the Choir. Eleven thousand, six hundred and two people, filed under *quiet* because you believed a voice cannot outlive the body that made it."

*The voice is a vibration. Vibration requires a medium. When the medium is removed—*

"When the medium is removed, the vibration goes somewhere else," I said. "That's what vibration does. It doesn't stop. It changes address."

I took my hand off the carved wolf and stepped back into the middle of the ruin. I drew the Discord up — not into a blade, not into a thread, but into a small, single note, the first phrase of the lullaby, the one my mother had sung over a cot before the sky broke. I held it in my chest for a heartbeat, feeling the old warmth, the familiar shape of a sound about to be released. Then I let it go.

It came out of me and went into the ruin. It bounced off the cracked altar, went up through the roofless air, and — I had expected it to die there, the way a note dies in an empty room — it did not die. It went into the stone. I felt it enter the carved wolf in the lintel, felt the three-hundred-year-old howl take the note the way a dry field takes rain, and then the note came back out of the stone and went on, into the wind, over the hill, toward the well.

I stood in the ruin with my mouth closed and my throat quiet and my chest empty of the note, and I heard the note singing anyway.

It was not an echo. An echo is a copy that fades. This was the note itself, still going, still changing shape as it went — the lullaby bending into a howl and the howl bending back into the lullaby, moving faster than I could have sung it, faster than any throat could have held it. I had let it go and it had kept going. I had stopped and it had not stopped.

The Voice was silent. Not the watchful silence between cycles. The silence of a machine that has just been shown a fact that does not fit in any of its columns.

"Do you see?" I said. "A blade stops when the hand stops. A thread stops when the thread runs out. But a note — a note that changes faster than you can write it down — has nowhere to stop. It just keeps changing address. Stone. Wind. Wheat. Whoever's throat is next."

I walked out of the ruin with the note still singing somewhere ahead of me, over the hill, toward the well. I had gone in looking for a hypothesis and had found something better. I had found proof. When the well took my body and filed it under *finalized*, the note would not be in the ledger. It would be in the stone, the wind, the wheat, the next throat. The machine would close its books on an asset that had already left the building.

That was the howling. That was the choice, stripped of the last of its disguise: not to die, but to become the thing that could not be made to stop.

*This constitutes a deviation,* said the Voice, and the sound of it was thinner now, as though the note going over the hill had taken some of the machine's certainty with it.

"Yes," I said. "It does."

---

Vark was waiting at the well.

He stood at the threshold of the chamber where the deep well opened into the world, a massive shape carved out of the grey light, his arms crossed, his bear-kin shoulders filling the space between the pillars like a yoke. He had been waiting for me. He had known where I would go. Vark knew the shape of my thinking the way a sculptor knows the grain of stone.

Since the forge, he had been different. I had broken him there — not his neck, which would have been simple, but his certainty. I had made him *hear*. For six weeks he had been walking around inside his own head with a hole in it where the Auditor's frequency used to be, and he had not forgiven me for it, and he had not been able to look away from me since.

He looked older than he had at dawn. The Vanguard had split three days ago — two hundred of them following me out of the muster, the rest staying with the lords and the empty certainty of the old orders — and the split had cost him something I could not name, the way a tree costs something when half its roots are cut. Those who had followed me had chosen the mistake over the order. I had not asked them to. They had watched me drill listening instead of killing for seven weeks and had made their own arithmetic, and Vark had watched them make it, and had not stopped them.

"The ones who followed you," he said. "What did you tell them?"

"The truth."

"That's a dangerous thing to give soldiers."

"It's the only thing they haven't been given." I looked past him at the well-mouth, at the blue light welling up through the ring of violet film. "They can go back, Vark. You tell them that for me. Anyone who wants to return to the lords' books can go, and no one will call them cowards for it."

"And the rest?"

"The rest can put names back. There's a child in the market who'll tell them how."

He grunted — not agreement, not disagreement, only the sound of a man putting a fact somewhere he could carry it. Then, after a moment: "You're going down," he said. "To break it."

"I'm going to try."

"Fenris." He almost never used the name. He had called me Shadowfang for seventeen years, the way the system had named me, the way a smith names a blade. The name in his mouth now was a small, strange thing, like a tool held wrong. "You think that if you give it yourself, it will choke. You think your frequency is strong enough to break the Choir from the inside."

"I am the highest resonance record in the western territories."

"You are a *man,*" Vark said, and the word came out of him like a stone dropped in water. "You are a man who is about to walk into the machine and let it take him apart, and you are telling yourself it is a tactic. It is not a tactic. It is a suicide with better bookkeeping."

"Suicide ends a story," I said. "This doesn't. That's the difference. The war keeps the doors open. The Choir feeds the doors. But the doors only hold while the machine can file the voices that hold them. I'm going to give it one it can't file."

"What story?"

"The one where the names go into the ledger and don't come out. The one where a child stands in a market and watches the people she loves keep standing after there's nobody home inside them. The one where my brother gets poured into a rift because his light is the last harmonic the lords need." I looked at the blue. "I can break the machine. I can't unwrite the story. But if I become the thing the machine can't file — the error it can't archive — then the story has a hole in it. And holes can be filled with something else."

"With what?"

I thought about the lullaby. I thought about the third phrase my mother could never quite reach. I thought about the child who had said *my name was Elin,* and the baker with ash to the wrist, and the eleven thousand names I had erased from a ledger because I had known, even then, that the harvest counted what it ate.

"With music," I said.

Vark was quiet for a long time. The blue light from the well washed over his face and turned the scars gray. When he spoke, it was not the clinical voice and it was not the voice of the trainer. It was something I had never heard from him — something tired, and old, and almost gentle, the way a stone is gentle after a thousand years of water.

"I taught you to compress the Noise," he said. "I taught you to turn the scream into a whisper and the whisper into a blade. I never taught you to let the scream be a scream."

"You didn't have to," I said. "You taught me to hear. That was enough."

He looked away — toward the well, toward the blue, toward the place where the deep frequencies waited to be fed. "If you go down there," he said, "there's no coming back up. Not as you. Not as a man with a name and a wound and a brother to go home to."

"I know."

"Then what is it you think you're choosing?"

I had been asked this question by the child in the market, and I had not been able to answer her. Vark asked it differently — not as an accusation, not as a test, but as a man who had spent seventeen years shaping a weapon and now wanted to know what the weapon had decided to become when his back was turned.

I looked at the well. The hum came up through the soles of my boots and into my bones and found the place where my blood met my bones, and the Discord there answered — not with the sharp, addictive warmth of the blade, but with something older. Something that had been in the ash of Oakhaven when I was eight and had hummed a lullaby no one was listening to. The singing was the thing. The thing was worth doing even if no one was listening.

"I'm choosing the howling," I said.

"The howling."

"Not the blade. Not the silence. The howling. The thing that's still a sound after the throat that made it is gone." I stepped past Vark, toward the well-mouth, and felt the blue light rise up to meet me. "You made me a weapon, Vark. I'm what happens when a weapon decides to sing."

I did not look back. But I heard him — a sound behind me in the dark, not a word, not a step, only the small, broken exhale of a man who had finally heard something he could not file under *viable* or *finalized* or any of the twenty-three words for compliance the Pits had taught him.

I went down the stairs into the well.


## Chapter 29: The Howling

The stairs went down further than a well had any right to.

They were cut into the black stone in a slow spiral, worn smooth by generations of keepers and chiurgeons who had come down to feed the frequencies and gone back up carrying the ledgers. I counted the steps because counting was mine. Three hundred and eleven to the first landing. Another two hundred to the second. Then the stone changed, and the blue began.

It did not begin as light. It began as cold — a cold that came up through the soles of my boots and climbed my legs and settled in my chest with the patient weight of a thing that had been waiting for me. Then the hum. Then the light, welling up out of the cracks between the stones, blue and without source, the color of a vein seen through thin skin. The deeper I went, the less the walls looked like stone and the more they looked like something grown — mineral cartilage, ridged and wet, with a faint pulse that traveled through it in time with the hum.


I had served it for seventeen years without ever seeing its mouth. Now I stood at the threshold of the lower chamber and looked in.

The Choir was hanging in the blue.

They were not bodies. That was the thing I had been dreading — not bones, not the remains of the eleven thousand anchors the harvest had consumed, but something worse. They were *voices*. Thousands of them, suspended in the well like dust in a shaft of light, each one a thin, glowing filament wound around the others in a vast, slow spiral. Some were bright and whole, caught in the act of a note. Most were frayed — thin as spider silk, broken at the ends, still humming the last fraction of a sound they had been making when the harvest took them. The Choir had no faces. It had no bodies. It was the part of a person that had been too stubborn to stop singing after everything else had been taken.


I had written half of them into the ledger with my own hand.

On a shelf cut into the chamber wall I found the slates — hundreds of iron tablets, intake manifests, every row ending in the same word cut deep enough to catch the light: FINALIZED. I turned one over. At the bottom of the second side, in the flat geometry of the system's script, was my own designation. SHADOWFANG. High-resonance Discord-carrier. Asset 4-1-7. I had never seen it on the intake side of a ledger before. I had always been the hand that wrote the word onto other people. I was in the book. Every asset ended in that column, and it was the only ending the ledgers had.

My hands were steady. The conditioning had taught me to hold a blade without trembling. It had not taught me what to do with a man who had finally read his own row.

"Look at them," said the Voice.


*Eleven thousand, six hundred and two. The Choir is at threshold. The Hero's light is the final harmonic. Deliver the vessel. Complete the Ascension. You were made for this.*

"Made for this," I said. My voice came back off the blue walls, flat and small.

*Every cut, every chamber, every name you erased — all of it was calibration. You are the hand that closes the circuit.*

I put my palm against the wall. The mineral cartilage was warm — the clinical warmth of a machine kept functional by not letting it freeze. Under my palm the filaments turned, and a sound came out of the blue that was almost a voice and not quite one. A woman humming. A child's laugh with the breath taken out of it. The third phrase of a lullaby — the one my mother had always reached for and never quite caught — rose through the well and pressed against the back of my teeth the way the Discord used to. I understood that my mother was in here.

Not her body. Not the light that had folded into the wheat. The *sound*. The part of her that had kept singing after everything else was ash. I had been carrying her in my chest for seventeen years without knowing the well had been carrying her too.


"Neutralize the deviation," said the Voice. "The asset is contaminated. The child. The brother. Recalibrate."

"There is no recalibration," I said.

*There is always recalibration. Sit. Receive the frequency. Become useful.*



"You think noise is a problem to be solved," I said to the Voice.

*Noise is a frequency without direction. It is wasted vibration. It is—*

"A song," I said.

The Voice stopped. The well went quiet in my skull in the way a room goes quiet when someone has said the unsayable thing and everyone is waiting to see whether the walls will hold.

I reached into my coat and took out the Oakhaven shard. It was warm — the warmth of memory, of hands that had held it before me, of a village that had been a village before the sky broke. The pale gold veins caught the blue light and turned it into a color that had no name. Now I knew why the Auditor had never made me destroy it: it was an address. A shard of the original anchor of Oakhaven, keyed to the blood of the people who had lived there. Lukas was the last of that blood the machine had not consumed. The shard was a line already drawn between the well and my brother. All I had to do was put a note on it.

I sat on the cold stone at the threshold of the Choir and began to compress.


I let the lullaby come.

*Hush now, little shadow, the moon's keeping score.*
*Close your eyes, little brother, I'll find you once more.*

The thread formed. It went out of my chest, through the shard, along the line of blood and ash and wheat that connected me to my brother, and I put the instruction on it — not words, not exactly; the machine did not use words and the thread would not carry them the way a man speaks. It carried the shape of an intention, the way music carries a meaning that has no translation. I made the shape three times, because there were three things, and I counted them because counting was mine.

One. Do not follow me down. There is no road where I am going, only the sound of one.

Two. The names I took out of the ledger — put them back. Every one. Speak them. Let them be spoken.

Three. When the light comes — and it will — spend it on the living. Not on me. Never on me.

Then, after the three, I put the thing that was not an instruction on the thread. The thing a machine could not file. I put my brother's face on it — the face in the wheat, the face in the cellar cave with the whetstone and the stick, the amber eyes that had not changed in seventeen years — and let the thread carry that too, because otherwise the three instructions would arrive as the Voice would have sent them: as a command, as data, as one more frequency in a life already too full of frequencies.

The thread went out. I felt it leave — a small, sharp loosening in my chest, like a stitch coming undone. I let it go and stopped looking at it. Looking at it was the same as holding it, and holding it would have pulled it back, and I had spent my whole life pulling things back — the blade, the kill, the warmth, the name — and I was finished with pulling things back. The machine's eyes slid over the thread the way a clerk's eyes slide over a line of poetry in a manifest: poetry is not a column, and therefore does not exist.

Above me, at the top of the stairs, there was a sound — small, light, a foot on stone trying very hard not to be a foot on stone.

I looked up. The child — Elin — stood at the threshold of the lower chamber, peering down into the blue with her amber eyes wide and her ragged coat held tight around her. She had followed me down three hundred and eleven steps and two hundred more, and had not made a sound until now.

"You shouldn't be here," I said.

"I know." She did not move. "I had to see where the names go."

"Now you've seen."


"Are those people?"

"Yes."

"Are they dead?"

"No." I looked at my mother's third phrase caught in the blue like a bird in a snare. "Not yet. That's the part I'm going to fix."

"How?"


"I'm going to sing back."

"Sing back?"

"The machine eats voices. It can't digest them, so it keeps them, and the keeping is the harvest, and the harvest feeds the war. You can't cut it. You can't burn it. It's been starving people for a hundred years and it's gotten very good at it. But it can only hold what it can file. A voice it understands, it keeps. A voice that changes faster than it can write it down —" I reached for the Discord in my chest, and it answered, warm and slow and ready "— it has to let go."

"So you'll be a voice it can't write down."

"Yes."

"Will it hurt?" she asked. The question a child asks when she already knows the answer and wants an adult to say it so she can decide whether to believe it.

"Yes," I said. "It will hurt."

"Will you come back?"

I could lie. The conditioning had taught me to lie with the flat precision of a machine reading a report. But she was the witness — the one who would stand here when the blue went out and carry what she had seen into the years after — and if I lied to her now, the lie would be the only thing left of me in her mouth.

"No," I said. "I won't come back."

She nodded once. Sharp. Downward. Like Vark. "Then I'll remember your name," she said, and turned and began to climb the stairs, and did not look back. I did not call after her. Calling after her would have meant never letting go of the well.



*You will not do this. You are a tool. Tools do not sing. Tools do not choose.*

"I know the line," I said. "I've heard it a hundred times."


I began to gather the howling.

It started in the place where my blood met my bones — the old place, the first place, the place the Auditor's fork had found when I was eight and the Null-Quartz walls had shuddered. The Discord rose, but not as a blade. It rose as a sound I had never made before: a long, low note with a fracture in it, the lullaby bent into a howl, the howl bent back into the lullaby, changing shape faster than the well could name it. I held it in my chest and let it grow, and the Choir turned toward it the way flowers turn toward a light that has just come on, and the blue in the well began to flicker.

Above me the doors of the lower chamber were opening. I heard boots — many of them — the measured tread of men sent to find out what had happened to the frequency. I did not look up. I held the howling and waited for them to come down.

They came in formation.

Nine Still Ones — the Auditor's correction-enforcers, silent, featureless, moving with the coordination of a single organism with nine sets of feet. They wore no armor and carried no weapons. They did not need them. A Still One did not cut you. It put its hands on you and made the frequency that constituted you go quiet, the way you pinch out a candle and move on. It was the machine's answer to a voice it could not file: not kill it, only stop it.

Behind them came four chiurgeons in gray robes, carrying slate ledgers, tuning forks, and the long needled instruments I had seen on the walls of the Pits when I was eight. And behind them — last, slow, filling the stairwell the way a storm fills a sky — came Vark.

I stood at the edge of the well with the howling held in my chest and watched them come.

"Shadowfang," Vark said. "Step away from the well."

I did not move.

"Step away," he said again, and there was something in it I had never heard — not the clinical flatness, not the trainer's authority, but the raw sound of a man who had spent seventeen years building something and was watching it walk into a fire.

"Vark," I said. "Look up."

He looked. He could not help it — seventeen years of following my eyes, of knowing that wherever I was looking was the thing that mattered. He looked past me into the well, at the Choir turning in the blue.

And he heard it.


"By the—" he said, and stopped.

The chiurgeons pushed past him. The Still Ones came down the last steps and spread across the chamber in a silent, widening ring. The lead one raised its hands. It had no face — only a smooth Null-Quartz oval — but I felt its attention focus on me the way the fork's frequency had focused in the chamber when I was eight. It was going to make me quiet. It was going to file me under *corrected* and move on to the child on the stairs and the brother down the line of the shard.

I let it come.


It was a death. I was choosing it. The difference between this and the kills I had made for seventeen years was the difference between a blade and a door: a blade goes out from you, a door opens and lets the world in. I was opening the door.

The Auditor came down on me in a wave.

*HALT. The asset is contaminated. Sit. Receive the frequency. You are a tool. TOOLS DO NOT—*

I detuned.

It was the thing I had taught myself in the borderlands — shifting my own frequency off the machine's note by a fraction, a quarter-tone, so the certainty slid past me like a hand through water. In the Null-Quartz chamber the Auditor had tuned me by pressing its frequency against mine until I matched. I had spent three weeks learning the opposite: how to hold a note that refused to match anything. I put the lullaby into the space where the command had been, and the command broke against it the way a wave breaks against a stone that does not move.

"Do you remember the third phrase?" I said to the Voice, quietly. "The one my mother could never reach? I think I can reach it now."

The Still One reached me.

Its hands came down on my shoulders — cold, smooth, without weight, the clinical grip of a thing that had done this to eleven thousand, six hundred and two people and never developed an opinion about it. The frequency it put on me was absolute: a flat, silent note that pressed against my chest and tried to make the Discord go quiet the way you close a fist around a bird.

For one heartbeat, it worked.

I felt the howling falter. I felt the old certainty rush back — the Voice, the blade, the usefulness, the warmth — and understood how the eleven thousand had been taken: not by force, but by this, this small cold pressure on the shoulders that says *stop singing, stop being a voice, become a number and be filed.* It was so gentle. That was the horror of it. So gentle, so efficient, so complete.

Then the Choir sang.

It was not a sound. It was eleven thousand, six hundred and two sounds, coming up through the well and through my boots and into the place where my blood met my bones, finding the howling where it had faltered and *joining* it. My mother's third phrase — the note she had reached for all her life and never quite caught — rose out of the blue and slid into the gap where my voice had nearly gone quiet, and it fit the way a key fits a lock that has waited a hundred years for exactly this tooth.

The stillness on my shoulders broke. The Still One's hands came off me. It staggered back, and the smooth oval of its face developed a hairline crack from crown to chin, the way the Auditor's mask had cracked in the chamber when I was eight. It was trying to file a sound it had no word for, to write down a note that changed shape between the striking and the recording. It could not.

I opened my mouth and let the howling out.


The chiurgeon dropped it and reached for a longer instrument, and I turned the howling on him and his frequency broke. He fell the way Raas had fallen — slowly, with the geological weight of a thing that has been rooted in a place and is now being asked to move. I counted. One. Two. Three. Four. The third died with his hands full of ledger pages that fluttered and tore in the wind of the howling; the fourth never made it to the steps.

The Still Ones came at me together — all nine, moving with the terrible coordination of a single organism, hands out, featureless faces turned toward me. They made no sounds. That was the thing about them: the machine's answer to noise was a thing that made no noise at all. But they had frequencies. Everything had a frequency. I had been trained to find them, the way a musician finds the note in a room, and in the blue of the well, with the Choir singing through my bones, I could not have missed them if I had tried.

I did not break them one at a time. I took them all at once, the way you strike a chord instead of a single note, and the chord found the nine flat silent notes and broke them together.

Five. Six. Seven. Eight. Nine.

They went down in a ring — nine bodies folding inward toward the well like nine petals closing — and the blue light caught the cracks spreading across their Null-Quartz faces and turned them into thin white lines, and the cracks reached the ends of the ovals and the ovals came apart and the things beneath them were — nothing. No faces. No bodies. The machine had never given them faces, only the smooth ovals and the flat notes and the patient hands, and when the notes broke there was nothing left to fold. Only nine sets of boots, falling over, and the silence where the hands had been.

Vark stood at the bottom of the stair.


"You could have stopped me," I said.

He shook his head. Once. A small, heavy movement.

"I tried," he said. His voice was barely a voice — scraped raw, the sound of a man who had spent seventeen years being certain and had just been given the gift and agony of hearing. "I tried to stop you when you walked past me on the stairs. And then I heard them. And I couldn't. Fenris — I couldn't."

He took a step toward me. The boots of the fallen Still Ones crackled under his weight. "Let me take your place."

The words stopped me more completely than the nine hands had. I looked at him — at Vark, my trainer, my maker, the man who had found a screaming child in the ash and decided he could be shaped into something useful.

"You can't. Your frequency isn't high enough. The machine would file you under *noise* and keep you." I looked past him, up the stairs, to the dark where Elin had gone. "Someone has to carry what happened here. You're the only one in Vaelthorne who can. When the lords send their questions, you'll answer them. When the child comes looking for a name to put on the ledger, you'll know it."

"Hold the line, Vark," I said, the way he had said it to me a thousand times in the training ring. "Just hold the godsdamned line."



I stepped into the well.

Not down into it. *Into* it. The blue came up around me like water — cold, mineral, alive with the turning of eleven thousand, six hundred and two filaments — and the Choir opened around my descending body the way a crowd opens for someone who has finally arrived. The voices pressed against me from every side. My mother's third phrase was there, and the child's laugh with the breath taken out of it, and a hundred thousand small ordinary sounds that had been people — a hammer on a roof, a spoon in a bowl, a woman calling a name across a yard — all of them caught in the blue, waiting for someone to sing them back into themselves.

The machine took me.

I felt it happen the way you feel a cold hand close on your wrist in the dark — not violent, not sudden, but absolute. It took my name first: *Shadowfang,* and the designation went out of me like a coat taken off in a warm room. Then the number — the high-resonance record, the viable asset, the tool — folded into the ledger with a small clean click I felt in my teeth. Then it took the body. The wound in my side — the one Thess had stitched three days ago, the one that had bled through the wrappings on the ride through the Greywood — opened and closed and opened again as the well's frequency rewrote the tissue around it. The body was being taken apart not by force but by *filing*: every piece of me assigned a category, a column, a place in the machine's vast and perfect accounting.

And through all of it — the name going, the number going, the body going — there was one thing the machine could not take.

The sound.


I was going into that hole.

I let the howling come up from the place where my blood met my bones — though my blood was being filed now, and my bones were being assigned their columns — and made it bigger than the well. A howl with a fracture in it, the fracture the lullaby, the lullaby a threat, the threat a promise, and the promise the only thing I had ever made that I had kept. I sang back at the machine with every voice it had ever tried to silence singing through me, and the howling went down into the blue and struck the Choir from the inside, and—

The Choir broke.

Not the well. Not the stone. The *Choir* — the eleven thousand, six hundred and two filaments the machine had strung up in the dark and made sing for a hundred years. My howling found the note they had all been singing, the note the machine had mistaken for obedience, and turned that note inside out. One by one, then in hundreds, then all at once, the filaments came loose.

They did not fall. They rose.

Eleven thousand, six hundred and two voices came apart out of the well like a handful of violet motes thrown into the air, and every one went back to being itself — the woman humming, the child's laugh, the hammer on the roof, the spoon in the bowl, the woman calling a name across a yard — and the sound of eleven thousand, six hundred and two people remembering who they had been went up through the chamber and through the stairwell and through the dark of Vaelthorne like a wave, and the blue light in the well flickered, and dimmed, and went out.

My mother's third phrase rose out of the Choir whole, at last, and finished itself, and I heard the last two notes of it without knowing how I knew them, and then they were gone up the stairwell with the rest, past the four dead chiurgeons and the nine unmade Still Ones and the man holding the line at the bottom of the stair, up to the dark at the top where a child waited to see whether the blue would go out.

It went out.

The well was dark. The deep frequencies were silent — not the silence of the machine, which is the silence of a ledger with everything in its place, but the silence of a room after a song has ended, which is a different silence entirely, and is full of the song.

The harvest broke. The doors stopped. Somewhere far above and far away the multiversal war lost its appetite, the way a fire loses its appetite when the fuel is taken out from under it, and the worlds held open by eleven thousand stolen voices began, slowly, to close.

I came out of the well.

Not climbing. There was no climbing. The machine had filed my body into its columns and the columns were empty now and the body had nowhere to go, so it did the only thing a body can do when there is nowhere left for it to be: it stopped.

I fell — not down, but outward — and felt the stone of the chamber catch me and hold me for the last time. The wound in my side was open and the blood was warm and the warmth was leaving it, going out into the cold air like breath on a mirror. My hands were gray with ash, the way they had been when I was eight and had climbed out of the crater in the Wastes. I had come full circle — the boy who woke in ash and was taken by men in iron masks was ending in a chamber of black stone with the ash on his hands and the song going up the stairs without him.

One body, I thought. Mine. That's the count.

Vark was beside me. I had not seen him come. He was on his knees now, his great hands hovering over me, not touching — the hands of a man who had been told by every lesson of his life that a weapon does not need to be caught, and had just learned, too late, that this one had wanted to be.

"Fenris," he said. The name again. It sounded different each time — more like a thing being learned than a thing being used.

I tried to answer. The howling had taken my throat and the silence had taken my breath, so I did the thing I had done since I was eight: I counted the things I could still feel. One, the cold of the stone under my back. Two, the warmth going out of the wound. Three, the sound of the voices going up the stairs. Four —

There was no four.

"Tell the child," I said. It came out as a whisper, a thread of air, the smallest sound I had ever made and the last. "Tell Elin — put the names back. All of them."

Vark bent his head. The great frame shook once. "I'll tell her."

The Voice came back one last time — not the Auditor's certainty, not the clinical command, but something small and thin and almost afraid, the sound of a machine looking at a ledger with a blank column where an asset used to be.

*Where,* it said, *did you go?*

I almost smiled. Seventeen years of reports and recalibrations and twenty-three words for compliance, and the machine's last question was the one it had never been able to answer for itself.

"Listen," I said. I had meant to tell it to listen to the song going up the stairs. But the word came out as a breath, and the breath went into the chamber, and the chamber was silent, and the song was already gone.

I closed my eyes. The body went still. The Discord went quiet in the place where it had lived since I was eight — not the quiet of the machine, not the quiet of the filed and the finalized, but the quiet of an instrument put down after a long piece of music, and at rest.

Above me, far up the spiral stair, a child stood in the dark and watched the blue go out, and did not look away. The song went up to her through the dark — eleven thousand, six hundred and two voices going past her like dust in a shaft of light, and one more voice under them that was not counted, that had never been in any ledger, that had no designation and no asset number and no column waiting with the word FINALIZED. She stood with her hand on the pale scar at her temple and listened to it go, and she was the only one who understood then that the machine had not been destroyed. It had been robbed. Eleven thousand, six hundred and three voices had been taken out of its account, and the account would never balance again.

And the howling went on without me.

## Chapter 30: What Remains

We woke in the wheat.

That is the only verb for it that is not a lie. We did not open eyes — there were no eyes to open. We did not draw breath — there was no breath to draw. We became aware the way a chord becomes aware of itself when the last note is struck and the sound is still in the air: all at once, in every place at once, with no center and no edge. One moment the well was dark and the body was still and the song was going up the stairwell without us. The next moment we were the song.

We were in the wind over the Oakhaven fields. We were in the crack in the mill stone where the rainwater went. We were in the gray loaves turning on the east row of Unity Market, in the spoon in a bowl three villages over, in the hammer on a roof in a town we had never been allowed to see. We were in a thousand, six hundred things at once, and none of them was a throat, and all of them were singing.

This was the thing we had not understood when we chose it: that a frequency which perpetually remakes itself does not stay where it is put. The machine had needed voices it could file — a note, a name, a column, a ledger. We had become the note that changed shape between the striking and the recording. We were unfileable. We were the error in the account. And because we were the error, we were everywhere the account could not reach.

It took us some time to learn the shape of this.

At first we tried to be a man. We reached for the old coordinates — the wound in the side, the cold of the stone under the back, the weight of a hand against a wall — and found nothing there to reach. There was no body. There was no *we* that owned a body. There was only the sound, and the sound went where the wind went, and the wind went where it wanted.

We tried to speak and had no mouth. We tried to see and had no eyes — only a thousand small, partial senses, the way a wall of water feels the stone around it. We could feel the wheat bending under the wind. We could feel the warmth of the loaves in the market. We could feel the thread we had sent down the line of the shard, still humming, still traveling, still carrying three instructions and a face toward the brother we had chosen as the address of our last act.

We could not hold anything. That was the first lesson, and it came without mercy.

We found him in the wheat.

Not at Vaelthorne. Not at a court, not at a throne, not at any of the places the machine had built to make waiting look like purpose. He had come home — to Oakhaven, to the field where the sky had broken when we were eight and the two of us had run through the gold with our mother's light at our backs. He stood in the middle of it, a man grown tall and broad, with silver-blond hair and the amber eyes that the years had not been able to touch, and he was very still.

We went to him the way sound goes to a place — not traveling, but arriving, the way a bell arrives in a valley. We came down through the wheat and settled around him in the air, and we waited for him to feel us.

He did.

A shiver went through him — not the cold, the wind was warm — a small, sharp lifting of the hair on his arms the way it lifts when someone stands too close behind you in the dark. He stopped. He looked around the field. There was no one there but the wheat and the hawk riding the thermals and the gold light going down over the hill.

"Fenris?" he said.

We answered. We could not make words, but we could make the wheat move — a single stalk, bending, then straightening — and we could make the sound that had been in our mother's third phrase, the two notes we had finally caught in the well, and we put them into the air around him as quietly as we could.

He closed his eyes. When he spoke again, his voice was rough.

"I know," he said. "I heard it in the market. Three days ago. The hum stopped. Everything — the wells, the walls, the frequency in the stone — it all just stopped. And there was this quiet. Like a door shut somewhere I couldn't find." He opened his eyes and looked at the wheat, at the single stalk still bending and straightening in the wind. "And then you. In my head. Not a voice. More like — a note. A note I almost remember."

We could not say *it is me.* We had no *me* to say it with. We were the note. We were the almost-remembering. We were the thing that had learned, in the space of three days and a hundred small arrivals, that we could brush against a mind the way a moth brushes a window, and no more.

We spent the next hour learning the edges of the thing we had become.

It was not an hour by any clock — the light did not move the way it had when we had eyes to watch it with — but it was an hour by the slow turning of the wind over the field, and that was enough. We pushed outward from the wheat to see how far we could reach. We went up into the hawk riding the thermals and found that we could feel the air under its wings but could not turn it. We went down into the hollow of a stone where a field mouse had nested and found that we could feel the warmth of its breathing but could not speak to it. We went into the well-song still hanging in the air over Vaelthorne and found that we could hear every note of it but could not add one.

We could perceive. We could not act. We could advise, in the narrow way a breeze advises a wheat field which way to bend, but we could not make a thing happen that the world had not already decided to happen.

This was the new arithmetic. We had been a blade — a thing that acted, that cut, that changed the shape of what it touched. Then we had been a note in a well — a thing that consumed and was consumed, that broke a machine by being broken by it. Now we were neither. We were the sound after the breaking, and a sound does not push. It does not pull. It goes where the air goes and settles where the air settles and waits to be heard by whoever happens to be listening.

We found Lukas again by following the thread.

It was still there — the line of vibration we had sent down the shard, ending in him. We went along it the way a hand goes along a rope in the dark, and we found that the thread did not end at his skin. It went into him, into the place behind his ribs where he carried the things he had never said out loud, and there it had become something we had not put there: a small, stubborn, unnameable certainty that he was not finished, that the line was still his to hold, that he would keep walking until the walking was done. We had sent three instructions and a face. He had made of them a reason to go on. That was the difference between what we had intended and what had arrived, and it was the difference between a frequency and a life.

We could not change it. We could not add to it or take from it. We could only be near it, the way a man stands near a fire he cannot touch, and feel the warmth go into him, and know that when he moved away, the warmth would stay with him and he would never know where it had come from.

That was advising. That was all we would ever do.

We tried to embrace him.

It is the honest thing to report, and there is no other way to report it. We gathered ourselves — all the scattered frequencies, all the motes in the wind and the wheat and the light — and we tried to come together into something that could hold him. A hand on a shoulder. An arm around a neck. The small, animal pressure of one body against another that says *I am here, you are here, and between us there is no distance.*

We could not do it. We came apart at the edges the way water comes apart when you try to hold it in your hands. We were in the wheat and the wind and the stone and the light, and each of those places was a separate place, and there was no way to be all of them at once in a single pair of arms. We had become the song. We were not the singer. And a song can go everywhere except into a person's arms.

Lukas felt the attempt. His breath caught. He put a hand out — not toward the wheat, not toward anything visible, but out into the empty air in front of him, the way a man reaches for someone who has just stepped around a corner. His fingers closed on nothing. He held them there for a moment, then let them drop.

"All right," he said. His voice was very quiet. "All right. I understand."

He did not understand. No one could have understood that quickly. But he was a man who had spent his whole life holding a line he could not see the end of, and he recognized the shape of it — that there was something here, and that it could not be held, and that the holding was not the point. The point was the line.

And then, from far off, we felt the Aegis change.

It was not a sound. It was a pressure — a slow, deep rearrangement of the frequency in the stone under the whole world, the way a great bell changes the air in a room before it is struck. We had no body to feel it with, but we had a thousand places to feel it in, and all of them felt it at once: the wells, the walls, the stone of the mill, the iron in the plow left in the field. Something enormous had moved through the world's armature, and the armature had not finished settling.

We knew what it was without being told. We were the error in the machine's account, and the machine was part of the world, and when a machine loses eleven thousand, six hundred and three voices in a single night, the whole armature rings. There would be men who would try to explain the pressure away — to call it a crowning, an accession, the world rearranging itself around a new shape of authority — and they would be right in the way men are right about the surfaces of things. We felt it from underneath. We felt it as a vibration in a wall, as a low chord of millions of people accepting a shape they had not chosen, as the sound of a crowd holding its breath and letting it out. We did not go to it. We were not allowed into that room. It was not our scene, and we had no body to bring into it even if it had been.

We only knew that the pressure passed, and the world settled by a fraction, and something in the frequency of everything had changed key and would not change back.

A figure came through the wheat from the direction of the village — small, ragged, with a pale scar running from temple to jaw and a slab of gray slate held against her chest with both hands. Elin. She had come. She had walked from the borderlands to Oakhaven with the ledger under her arm and the names in her head, and she had been looking for the man who had stood in the market and taken her name off the list.

She stopped when she saw Lukas. She did not know him. He did not know her. They stood in the wheat and looked at each other with the wary, unselfconscious attention of two creatures who have both been told, in different ways, that the other is dangerous.

"You're him," she said.

"I'm who?"

"The one he sent the note to." She lifted the slate. It was a Vaelthorne slate, the kind the lady with the slate had carried in the market, its surface covered in the geometric script that had no word for mercy and twenty-three for compliance. But the script on this one had been crossed out, line after line, and rewritten in a child's rough hand. "I took it from the well," she said. "After the blue went out. The men with the hammers were gone and the chiurgeons were gone and there was a big bear-kin sitting on the stairs with his head in his hands, and he told me to take this and put the names back. All of them. He said it was the last thing someone asked him to say."

Lukas looked at the slate. Then at her. "Who asked him?"

Elin's amber eyes went to the wheat — to the single stalk still bending, to the air where we were, to the place where the sound had been. She had an animal's sensitivity to frequencies; she had learned it in the market, standing in the ruins with the empty people still standing around her. She had heard the howling in the well. She had followed it up the stairs and out into the world, and she had carried it here.

"I don't know," she said. "He never told me his name."

Lukas was quiet for a long time. Then: "He didn't tell you his name."

"No. I asked. I said, 'What's your name, so when I say it it's a name and not a number.' And he said —" She stopped. Her hand went to the pale scar on her face. "He said, 'You first.' And I told him, Elin. And he said it back. 'Elin.' Like it was a thing he was going to keep." She looked at Lukas. "And then he went down the well and the blue went out and he didn't come back up."

A stillness went through Lukas — the stillness of a man who has been holding a question in his chest for seventeen years and has just been handed the answer in a form he cannot recognize.

We wanted to put our name into the air. Not as a frequency. Not as a note. As a word. *Fenris.* Our name. The name our mother had given us and our brother had used and Vark had set aside for seventeen years and the machine had tried to file under *designation.* We wanted to say it to the child who had asked for it and the man who had been waiting for it.

We could not. We had no throat. We had no tongue. We had only the song, and the song had no word in it that either of them would understand as a name.

We sent the lullaby instead.

It went out through the wheat — the whole field bending at once, the gold heads turning toward the two figures standing in the middle of it, the two notes of the third phrase riding the wind like a bird released from a snare. Elin's amber eyes widened. Lukas closed his again, and a single tear went down his face, and he did not wipe it away.

"Hush now," he said, very quietly. Not singing. Saying the words the way you say the thing you have been afraid to say out loud for seventeen years. "Little shadow. The moon's keeping score." He opened his eyes and looked at the wheat. "Close your eyes, little brother. I'll find you once more."

He said the last line wrong. It was not the line we had sent. It was the line he had needed to hear, the one he had been carrying since the day the Rift took us, the promise the brother who had been left behind had made to himself in a field of gold. *I'll find you once more.*

We could have told him. We could have put the correct note into the wind — the note we had caught in the well, the one that finished the phrase — and let him hear the whole song at last. But the correct note was a kind of goodbye, and he had just said the wrong line, and the wrong line was a kind of hope, and we discovered in that moment that we could advise but we could not comfort, and that the difference between the two was the whole of what we had lost.

"They're going to ask you to pay for it," Elin said.

Lukas looked at her. "Who is?"

"The lords. The ones who sent the men with the hammers." She lifted the slate, and the crossed-out script caught the light. "The blue went out. The wells are dead. The harvest stopped. That's a debt in their book, and debts get paid. He knew that. Before he went down. He told me someone had to carry what happened, and he told the bear-kin to hold the line, and he told me to put the names back. He knew they'd come looking."

Lukas looked at the slate. Then at the horizon — past the wheat, toward the roads that led east, toward the pressure we had just felt in the Aegis, toward the shape the world was settling into. He did not want it. We knew that about him — we had always known it, even from the other side of a Rift, even with the Auditor's certainty in our skull. Lukas had never wanted the line. He had only ever wanted the wheat, and the field, and the two of us small enough to be held in the frame of the gold.

"You should go," he said to Elin. "Take the slate. Go somewhere the lords don't have names for."

"No." She said it the way a child says no — flat, final, with the whole weight of a small creature's unshakable certainty behind it. "He told me to put the names back. That means saying them. Out loud. To people. I'm going to say them to everyone who'll listen, starting with the ones in the market who are still standing empty." She looked up at him. "You should come too."

"I'm not a singer."

"You don't have to sing. You just have to say the names." She shifted the slate against her hip. "That's what he said, isn't it? Put the names back."

We felt the note go through Lukas — the instruction we had sent down the thread, the second of the three, arriving at last in the form of a child's rough handwriting on a stolen slate. *Put them back. Every one. Speak them. Let them be spoken.* He had heard it three days ago as a note in the air. Now he heard it in Elin's voice, and it landed differently, because a child saying a thing with a slate under her arm is a fact, and facts were the only things Lukas had ever trusted.

He knelt in the wheat. He put one hand on the slate, over Elin's hand, and he looked at the crossed-out script and the names written in the rough child-hand beneath it.

"All right," he said. "Show me."

And we — we who could not hold him, who could not speak our name, who could not finish the phrase — we did the one thing we could do. We advised. We put the wind into the wheat so that the gold heads bent toward the slate, and we put a small, steady note into the air around the two of them — not the lullaby, not the howling, but something between, a low hum that was the sound of a line holding — and we knew, with the terrible clarity of a thing that has learned the limits of its own nature, that Lukas would feel it as his own conviction and would never know it was us.

They read for a long time. The light went down over the field and the gold turned to brass and then to a color that was not a color at all, and still they read. Elin's voice was flat and tireless. Lukas's voice was rougher, and he stumbled once over a name in the old elven script and asked her how to say it, and she told him, and he said it again until he got it right. We carried each name up into the wind as it was spoken, and held it there, and let it go only when the next one came.

We had expected the names to be heavy. They were not. A name spoken aloud weighs almost nothing — a breath, a shaping of the lips, a small vibration in the air — and yet the eleven thousand, six hundred and two of them, going up over Oakhaven in the space of an evening, weighed more than the well had ever weighed, more than the machine, more than the hundred years of ledgers built to contain them. The child had understood this before any of us. She had carried the slate across the borderlands because a name on a slate is a thing that can be read, and a name read is a thing that exists, and a thing that exists cannot be filed under FINALIZED without someone noticing the lie.

Near the end of the list, Lukas stopped.

"There's one here that isn't like the others," he said. His finger was on the last line of the slate, at the bottom, under the eleven thousand, six hundred and two. The rough child-hand had written a name there that the machine's script had never touched: FENRIS.

Elin said nothing. She had known it was there. She had written it herself.

"He was one of them," Lukas said. His voice was very quiet. "He was in the book the whole time. Asset four-one-seven. I said his name to the wheat three days ago and I didn't know he'd been in a ledger all my life."

"He wasn't in it at the end," Elin said. "I wrote him at the bottom, under the others. That's the right place. That's where the names go when they come back."

Lukas looked at the name for a long time. We were in the wind over his shoulder, in the gold bending around him, and we felt him reach for us the way he had reached in the field — not with his hand this time, but with something else, something that went down into the place behind his ribs and came up empty. He could not feel us the way a man feels a presence in a room. He could only feel the thread, and the note, and the certainty that had grown there since the market.

"He's here," he said. Not a question. A statement of the kind a man makes when he has stopped requiring proof.

"Where?"

Lukas looked at the field. At the wheat. At the air above it that had no shape and no color and no sound that a normal ear could have heard. "Everywhere," he said. "Nowhere I can put a hand on. That's — that's the way it is with some things." He closed the slate and handed it back to Elin. "Keep reading tomorrow. I have to go in the morning."

"I know."

"There's a debt coming due. The lords will send for the borderlands first, and after that they'll send for us, and there won't be a choice about the line." He stood, brushing the chaff from his knees. "I wanted you to know where the names are. If I don't come back—"

"You'll come back."

He almost smiled. It was not a happy smile. It was the smile of a man who has heard the lullaby and knows the last line was wrong and has decided to keep walking anyway. "Don't put words in my mouth, child. I'm not my brother. I don't come back from things."

We felt the note go through him — the low, steady hum we had put in the air, the sound of a line holding — and he mistook it, as we had known he would, for his own conviction. He straightened. He looked at the road east. He did not look back at the wheat.

One name. Then another. Then another.

We counted them. Counting had always been ours — first the lashes in the cell, then the kills in the ring, then the names in the ledger, and now these, going out over a field of gold in the last light of a day that would be remembered, if it was remembered at all, as the day the humming stopped. We had counted every kind of thing a man can count. We had never, until now, counted the things that were being given back.

## Chapter 31: Open Frequency

We walked the borderland roads.

We had walked them once as a weapon. We knew the grade of every hill on the road from Millhaven to the Greywood, the exact spot where the ruts held water after rain, the overhanging branch that had once snagged a black mare's harness and made me curse in a language the Pits had taught me. I had ridden that road with a wound in my side and the Voice in my skull and a mission packed into my coat like a knife. Now we went over the same ground as music — no boots, no saddle, no wound, no mission — and the road did not know the difference.

Or perhaps it did. The stone held the shape of us in a way it had never held the shape of any other traveler, because we had become the frequency of the road itself. We were in the ruts and the ditch water and the fence posts and the wind in the dead grass. We were in the place where a man's boot had pressed astone into the mud a hundred years ago and the stone had stayed pressed. We went where the road went, and the road went everywhere, and we were never still and never tired and never alone, because a song is never alone in a world that is full of things waiting to be heard.

It was different, being music on a road instead of a blade. A blade sees a road as a line to the target. Music sees it as the space between sounds. We heard the whole borderland as a score: the creak of a cart axle three fields over, a dog barking at nothing, the low hum of a dead well under a hill, the small high notes of insects in the grass. Eleven thousand, six hundred and three voices had gone out into this score on the night the well broke, and the world had not known what to do with the extra sound. It was still learning. We were still learning. We were the part of the song that had learned to walk.

---

We came to the waystation on the third night.

Nyssa's waystation sat where the borderland road met the old track to the Wastes, a low stone building with a slate roof and a single lamp above the door. We had been here once as weapon and once as co-conspirator — the quiet contact under suspicion, the woman who had agreed to listen without asking questions and had therefore become dangerous to both sides. I had left her with a warning and a half-truth and the kind of silence that keeps two people alive. Now we came back as the thing neither side had predicted.

A Vaelthorne examiner was questioning her when we arrived.

We found them in the front room — Nyssa behind the counter, a tall man in the gray robes of the audit standing in the middle of the floor with a slate in his hand. The examiner's back was to the door. His voice was flat. Clinical. The voice of someone reading a report.

"You were seen speaking to an unregistered frequency on the fourteenth," he said. "The border warden logged a deviation in this sector the same night. You will account for both."

"I sell bread and bed space to people who come through here," Nyssa said. Her voice was steady. "If you want me to account for every person who's spoken in my house, you'll need a bigger slate."

"Do not be clever. Cleverness is a form of deviation." The examiner turned a page. "There are reports of singing along this road. Travelers have described —"

"I haven't heard singing."

"Travelers are unreliable. Travelers are also expendable." He looked up. His eyes were small and pale. "You are not, yet. Answer the question."

We were in the lamp above the door, in the flame, in the small pool of light it threw across the counter. We could not speak to her. We could not have spoken to the examiner if we had wanted to — we had no mouth, and the flattest voice in Vaelthorne would have had nothing to listen to. But we could move the flame. We could make the light lean. We put a small, steady note into the lamp — the third phrase, the two notes we had caught in the well — and the flame bent toward Nyssa, away from the examiner, and held there.

Nyssa's hand tightened on the counter. Her eyes went to the lamp, then away again, too fast for the examiner to follow. She had learned to listen in a world that punished listening. She heard us the way she had heard the deviation on the fourteenth — not as a voice, but as a pressure, a small wrongness in the air that meant someone was trying to tell her something.

"There's a draft," she said. "The lamp's been doing that all week."

The examiner looked at the lamp. The flame straightened — we made it straighten, because a flame that keeps leaning becomes evidence — and he looked back at her.

"A draft," he said.

"A draft."

He wrote something on the slate. The scratch of the stylus went through the room like a small animal in distress. Then he closed the slate and tucked it under his arm.

"Stay available," he said. "The audit is not over. The harvest has been — interrupted. There will be questions." He turned to go. At the door he paused. "If you hear anything, you will report it. Including singing."

"Of course," Nyssa said.

He left. The door closed. The lamp burned on above the counter, and we were in it, and Nyssa stood very still beneath us with her hand flat on the counter and her head slightly tilted, the way a creature listens to a sound it cannot quite place.

"All right," she said, to the empty room, to the lamp, to us. "All right. I hear you. Whatever you are. I don't want to know." She wiped her hands on her apron. "But if you're the one who was here before — if you're the one who told me to listen and then went and got yourself turned into a story — then you could at least have the decency to stop haunting my lamp. I've got a business to run."

We could not laugh. We had no breath to laugh with. But we made the flame give one small, bright nod, and Nyssa saw it, and something went out of her shoulders — not relief, not fear, but the small, stubborn settling of a person who has decided that the impossible thing in front of her is, for tonight, manageable.

"Fine," she said. "Stay out of the drafts, then."

---

On the road past the waystation we came upon a patrol.

There were four of them — Vaelthorne infantry, young, their armor patched and their faces drawn with the particular exhaustion of men who have been marching toward a war that has not yet given them anything to do. They had stopped at a dead well beside the road, not to drink — there was nothing in it — but because a well is a place where a man can stand still for a moment without looking like he has stopped. One of them, the youngest, was kneeling at the stone mouth, running his fingers over the violet film that lined the inside.

"It's cold," he said. "Even in the sun. Feel it."

The others did not feel it. They were watching the road, the way men watch a road when they have been told that something might come down it.

"That's the last of them," said the one who seemed to be in charge. A sergeant, perhaps, though the war had not yet made the rank mean much. "Wells all the way to the border. Dead as stones. Nobody knows why."

"I know why," said the youngest. He was still kneeling at the well. "My sister was in Unity Market. She says a man came through and took the names out of a book. And then the ground shook and the people standing next to her went empty, and she says the man had something to do with it."

"Your sister talks too much."

"She talks because she's the only one left who remembers their names." The young soldier's voice cracked on the last word. He was perhaps nineteen. Perhaps less.

We were in the wind over the well, in the thin cold coming up out of its mouth, in the violet film under the young soldier's fingers. We could not speak to him. We had learned that. But we could be near him, and we could put a small, steady note into the air around his kneeling body — the low hum that was the sound of a line holding — and we did, and the young soldier stopped moving his fingers and held his hand very still on the stone.

He hummed.

It was the lullaby. Not the whole of it — three notes, the second phrase, bent and wrong and half-remembered — but it was the lullaby, and it came out of him without his knowing why, the way a man hums a song his mother sang when he is frightened and does not want the others to hear that he is frightened. We had not put the song into him. We had never done that. We could not. We had only made the space around him quiet enough that the song already in him could be heard.

The sergeant turned. "What was that?"

"Nothing."

"You were singing."

"No." The young soldier stood. His hand came off the well mouth. He looked down at it as though it had done something without his permission. "I don't sing."

The sergeant stared at him a moment longer, then turned back to the road. "Keep moving. The muster's in three days and I don't want to be late because you were talking to a well."

They went east. We went with them for a while, in the wind around the young soldier's head, and then we were not, and we had not been anywhere else, and we had not changed a single thing he would do. We had made a space. He had filled it with the only thing that could have filled it. That was the whole of what we could do now, and it was enough — it had to be enough — because it was all there was.

---

We found the wolfpack two nights later, camped in the lee of a broken stone wall on the road to the finalized zones.

Two hundred of them had followed us out of the muster. They had chosen the mistake over the order, and they had been living with the choice for seven weeks, and we had felt them on the road for days without being able to do anything about the shape of their doubt. Now we came into their camp as the wind in the dead grass and the small sound of the fire taking.

They were mostly young. Vaelthorne's finest, Vark had called them — but finest had meant sharpest, and sharpest had meant most easily cut. They sat around the fire in a ring, and their armor was patched with cloth, and their weapons were cleaned with the absent, repetitive care of men who needed something to do with their hands. One of them — a woman with a shaved head and a scar across her nose — was sharpening a blade that did not need sharpening. Another, younger, was writing something on a scrap of slate with a stylus that shook slightly.

"There's no word from Vaelthorne," the young one said. "That's the third messenger we've sent."

"There won't be," said the woman with the scar. "Vaelthorne doesn't answer deserters."

"We're not deserters."

"Then what are we? The lords' books say we're gone. The Auditor's ledger says we're noise. And here we sit, two hundred of us, with no orders and no pay and no idea what we're waiting for." The woman stopped sharpening the blade. She looked at it, then put it down. "He told us we could go back. Did you hear him? 'Anyone who wants to return can go, and no one will call them cowards.'"

"I heard him."

"So why is none of us going?"

No one answered. We were in the fire with them, in the small sparks going up into the dark, in the wind that moved the smoke across the ring. We had no voice. We had no orders to give — we had spent seventeen years obeying orders and one week refusing them, and we had no idea how to give one. But we had the low hum, the sound of a line holding, and we put it into the fire so that the flames leaned into the ring, toward the center where the men sat, and the two notes of the third phrase went out with it.

The young one with the slate stopped writing. He looked around the ring. "Did you hear that?"

"Hear what?"

"That — " He made a small sound with his mouth, trying to reproduce it, and failed. "Like a hum. Under the fire."

The woman with the scar said nothing. She was looking into the flames with an expression we recognized because we had seen it on Vark's face at the bottom of the well — the expression of a person who has spent a lifetime being certain and has just been given the agony and the gift of hearing something she cannot file.

"Put the blade down," she said, after a while. Her voice was different. "All of you. Put them down." She picked up the slate from the young man's lap and looked at what he had been writing. It was a list of names — the names he had been scratching onto the slate in the dark, the names from the market, the ones the child in Unity Market had been reading aloud. "He told us to put the names back," she said. "I thought he meant it as a metaphor. I don't think he did."

"What do we do with them?"

"Say them." She handed the slate back. "To anybody who'll listen. Starting with each other."

They sat in the ring around the fire and began to say the names, awkwardly at first, stumbling over the ones they could not pronounce, and the sound went up into the dark and joined the other sound — the eleven thousand, six hundred and three voices going out over the borderlands — and we carried both of them, and held them, and let them go into the wind together.

We had advised. They had heard us as themselves. That was the whole of it. That was all a song could do.

---

At dawn the wolfpack broke camp and we went with them as far as the crossroads.

They were quieter than they had been the night before. The names had done something to them — not made them gentle, not made them good, but given their hands a thing to hold that was not a weapon, and the difference showed in the way they moved. The woman with the scar carried the slate at the front of the column instead of at her belt. The young man who had been writing walked beside her with his own stylus, and now and then he would say a name aloud, and she would repeat it, and the column would take it up one voice at a time until the whole road was carrying it. We went along the line and through the sound, and it was a strange thing to be inside a song that was being sung by the very men who had once been trained to be silence.

At the crossroads they turned south, toward the borderland settlements the lords would blame first, and we turned north, because the north was where the stone was thin and the old frequency ran close to the surface, and we had learned that we were drawn to thin places the way water is drawn to a crack. We had no body to turn. We went where the song went, and the song went where it was needed, and in the weeks that followed we learned to read the shape of a place by how badly it hurt.

There was a town on the north road that had been finalized in the third year of the war. We found a family living in the shell of its granary — a man, a woman, two children, their belongings in a cart with one wheel bound in wire. They were not hiding. They were waiting, the way people wait when they have nowhere left to go and have decided that waiting is a kind of occupation. The man was mending a fishing net that had no water within ten leagues. The woman had laid out a cloth on a stone and was arranging on it the small, useless objects they had carried out of their house: a cracked bowl, a child's shoe, a piece of carved wood, a slate with a single name on it.

The younger child came to the edge of the road and stood looking north, where the old rift-scar showed purple in the sky. We were in the wind around her, and we made the wind move the hem of her coat, and she looked down at it.

"Mama," she said. "The wind's here again."

"It's always here," said the woman, without looking up from the cloth. "Leave it alone."

"It sounds like — " The child made a small sound with her mouth, trying to find the shape of it, and failed. "Like when the lady reads the names."

The woman's hands stopped on the cloth. She looked at her daughter for a long moment, then went back to arranging the cracked bowl, the shoe, the piece of wood, the slate. "You don't remember the lady," she said. "You were too little."

"I remember the names."

"Then keep them." The woman's voice was flat, but her hands were not — they trembled once, and then were still. "That's all anybody can do. Keep them and say them when somebody's listening."

We stayed with them until the light went. We could not help them mend the net or fix the wheel or find water in a dry land. We could not tell them the wells were dead because of what we had done, or that the war that had taken their town had lost its doors because a man had sung himself into a well and not come out. We could only be in the wind around the child and in the small cold place between the cracked bowl and the carved wood, and we put a low note into the air that was not a comfort and was not an answer, and the child stopped trying to say the sound and simply stood in it until her mother called her in.

That was the new life. To be near and not to help. To be heard as the wind and called the wind. To go on.

We went north, and the world changed key again.

It happened on a night when the road ran high above the finalized zones, where the stone was thin and the frequency underneath ran close to the surface. We were in the stone, so we felt it before anything else — before the air, before the sound, before any creature with ears could have known that something was happening.

A golden pulse went through the Aegis.

Not a sound. A pressure, and then a color we did not have eyes to see but felt the way a man feels warmth on his face with his eyes closed — a deep, golden vibration moving through the armature of the world from somewhere far to the east, and a silence behind it. The silence was the terrible part. It was not the silence of the machine, which is the silence of a ledger with everything in its place. It was the silence of a room after a great voice has stopped speaking, when the air itself seems to be holding still out of respect. Something enormous had passed out of the world, and the world had gone quiet to listen to the space where it had been.

We knew what it was without being told. We had no name for it that belonged to us, and we did not try to enter it. We had learned our limits in the wheat with Lukas: we could advise, we could never embrace; we could be near a thing, we could never be its witness in the way a body is a witness. A passing like this — a golden pulse through the Aegis, a silence where a frequency had been — was not ours to stand beside. It belonged to the ones who had lived with the dragon in the world, who had known the shape of him while he was still there to be known. We felt it the way you feel a funeral procession go past a house you are not invited into — the slow tread, the quiet, the pressure of something too large to carry being carried by other hands.

We did not go to it. We only lowered our sound, the way a traveler lowers his voice at a passing burial, and went on.

And the world rearranged around a crown.

We had felt the first pressure of it in the wheat three nights ago — the low chord of millions of people accepting a shape they had not chosen. Now, from the high road above the finalized zones, we felt it settle. There was a ceremony happening somewhere to the east, in a city of stone and banners and people who had spent their lives pretending the world was whole. We did not see it. We were not allowed to see it. We felt it the way you feel a crowd through a wall — a vibration in the stone, a sound like a great many people holding their breath and letting it out together, the low, deep thud of something being accepted that could not be unaccepted. The Aegis rang with it. The wells, long dead, hummed with a sound that was not their own. And under all of it ran the smaller sound — eleven thousand, six hundred and three voices being spoken aloud in markets and camps and waystations by people who had never met them — and we understood that these two things were happening at the same time and that the world would never be the same because of either of them.

We did not enter that scene either. It was not ours. We had no body to bring into it, and no name to speak in it, and no right to stand in the light of a thing we had only ever known from underneath. We were the howling shadow. We belonged to the space beneath the ceremonial floor, to the vibration in the stone that the people in the room would never know was there. We went on.

---

We passed the graveyard of anchors on the fifth night.

We had mapped it as a weapon — a valley of consumed places where the harvest had finalized whole settlements and left the silence behind like a trail of dropped breadcrumbs. We had walked it with a slate and a measuring thread and counted what was missing. Now we went through it as the thing that had come back for what was taken.

The motes were there.

Eleven thousand, six hundred and three points of violet light, hanging over the valley in the dark, each one a voice that had been filed and unfiled, each one going back to being itself at its own pace — some quick, some slow, some still caught in the last fraction of a sound they had been making when the harvest took them. We moved among them and they moved with us, the way dust moves with a hand passing through it, and we felt the old frequency of the place trying to reassert itself — the machine's habit, the centuries of muscle memory in the stone — and we put our sound into it, and the stone let go, the way a fist lets go when the thing inside it is no longer there to be held.

We counted them as we went. We had counted them as a blade, and we counted them now as music, and the two counts were the same number, which was the only justice the world had ever offered us that we could actually hold.

The road went on. We went with it.

We were not going anywhere. That was the last lesson, and it came without ceremony. A wandering song does not travel toward a destination — it travels because the air moves, and the air moves because the world turns, and the world turns whether anyone is listening or not. We had spent seventeen years being pointed at targets.
